One door still does not enforce it: the offline replica holds only the actor's own membership, so it throws — a **named follow-on on the roster's role control**, recorded in `test-cases/13`. `packages/rule-engine/src/checks/membership.ts`, `apps/server/src/engine-context.ts`

- ✅ **Only an owner may grant the owner role** — the ACTOR half of the last-owner guard, and the half that closes an escalation the world half cannot. `membership.granted { role: 'owner' }` is refused with `grant-owner-requires-owner` unless the session user's own workspace role **is** owner, so an admin may promote somebody to admin, member or guest but cannot mint an owner — themselves included. It is a comparison written inline against the `role` already in scope, not a second read: the gate computes `isWorkspaceAdmin = role === 'owner' || role === 'admin'` and nothing narrower, so there is no helper to reuse. A payload that does not parse is deliberately **not** refused here — it falls through to the engine's `malformed-payload`, which names the offending field, because a shape problem and an authority problem have different remedies. **The hole this plan exists for was here:** `POST /events` is the client-submitted door, its body schema is unconstrained by event type, and `membership.granted` was admin-gated and nothing more — so before this, any workspace admin could demote the sole owner and promote themselves, in two events, with the projection overwriting `role` on conflict. The rule id is its own rather than the generic `permission.role` because the members page renders the rule string and *"only an owner can hand over the workspace"* is not the same thing to be told as *"only an admin can manage membership"* — the same argument that gave `permission.visibility` its own id. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ✅ **Only an owner may REMOVE the owner role — the mirror of the rule above** (owner ruling 2026-09-18, second sitting: *"may an admin demote a non-last owner: **no**"*). Changing any member's role **to or from** `owner` is an owner-only act; an admin still moves members between `admin`/`member`/`guest`, unchanged. **The two shipped rules each closed half a door and the halves did not meet:** `grant-owner-requires-owner` refuses a payload that GRANTS `owner`, and `last-owner` refuses a change that would leave the workspace with ZERO owners — so an admin demoting an owner *while another owner remained* was refused by neither, and `proj_memberships`' `ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE SET role = excluded.role` would write it. Measured before the fix, through the client door: the event **committed**. `checkMembershipOwnerAuthority` runs as the gate's **second** pass — after the pure role pass, so a claim from below admin is still answered without a lookup, and before the project/visibility passes — and refuses with `remove-owner-requires-owner`. It is the first pass that reads, because the ruling is about the ACTOR but its premise is about the TARGET: the actor's role is already resolved (`ctx.eff.workspaceRole`), while "is this a move *from* owner?" needs the target's row, read through `resolveEffectiveRole` (the module's one safe reader) rather than a bare `SELECT`. **It does not replace `last-owner` and must not** — an owner demoting themselves out of the last ownership passes this gate and is refused by the engine's count, so each rule stays reachable and each has its own case. The members roster needs no change: `EDITABLE_ROLE_OPTIONS` (`apps/web/app/w/[workspaceId]/members/page.tsx:546-550`) omits `owner` and its `<Select>` is gated on `isEditableRole`, so an owner's row renders a chip — the control cannot build such a payload from either side. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`, `packages/rule-engine/src/refusal-rules.ts`
- ✅ **Ingest enforces visibility: nobody may act on a ticket they cannot see** — `checkVisibilityPermission` runs inside the same gate, after the role pass, and defers every audience question to `canSee`. A guest acting on a ticket no explicit list names them in is refused `permission.visibility`; so is a member reaching into another project's ticket by direct id — the same probe Task 8 answers with an indistinguishable 404, met at the write door. **A ticket that never existed and one the actor may not see answer identically**, because `canSee` cannot tell them apart and giving them different sentences would put the oracle the 404 discipline removed one layer down. A guest's write ceiling needs no role branch: it falls out of `canSee`, whose guest answer is explicit-only. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ✅ **Who may retarget a ticket's audience, and who may create one** — `visibility.set` demands **project-admin rights on the ticket's own project or ticket creatorship**, and a claim whose `payload.ticketId` disagrees with the entity it is filed against is refused **before anything is looked up**: it does not name the ticket it rides on. `ticket.created` demands membership of the target project with **no admin shortcut** — an admin joins the project first, an attributed event. Both report `permission.role`, and both run in the visibility pass rather than the pure role check, each moved there by a constraint rather than a preference: the project-membership question must follow the project-existence check so the honest reason wins, and the authority question must follow `canSee` or it becomes an existence oracle. Creatorship is read from the **ledger** — `events.actor_id` on the ticket's own `ticket.created` row — because no projection carries it and inventing one would be a competing definition of Plan 3's head state. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ⚙️ **Which projects exist (`proj_projects`)** — a projection of `project.created`, registered at its coordination-§8 position (fourth, before `proj_tickets`) rather than appended, because the ticket precondition reads it. `checkProjectExists` refuses any event **naming** a project the workspace never founded, and covers the roster events as well as `ticket.created` — validating only ticket creation leaves the hole open on the side it was actually reachable from, since `project.member_added` accepts any ULID as a scope, and a membership granted on a typo'd id used to get the ticket committed with a dangling `project_id`, on no board and in no project list. `projectExists` is **scoped by workspace**, so a real project id asked under the wrong workspace is simply not found. **Task 16 (2026-09-09) added `archived`** — a boolean column folded from a NEW symmetric event pair, `project.archived` / `project.restored`, both filed against the project entity with EMPTY `.strict()` payloads (the project is `entity.id`, so a payload naming one is refused rather than ignored). A pair rather than one event carrying a boolean: a re-delivered toggle would flip a project back, and `project.restored` reads honestly in a history list. Both are in `ADMIN_ONLY_TYPES` — retiring a project is the workspace's decision, not a project admin's roster power — and both are in `projectsNamedBy`, so they inherit the existence precondition and an archive naming an unfounded project is told the project does not exist. **Archiving is not a delete and not a permission:** nothing in `permissions/` reads the column, no membership changes, `projectExists` still answers true, and the invitation gate's dependency on a founded project staying founded is untouched. It decides only where a project is LISTED. `apps/server/src/projections/proj-projects.ts`, `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`, `apps/server/migrations/1788700000000_proj-projects-archived.sql`, `packages/domain/src/events/membership.ts`
- ⚙️ **Two doors, and which one a caller uses is the design** — `ingestClientBatch` is gated; `ingestBatch` is the internal append path and is not, so the server's own legitimately server-origin appends reach the ledger without knocking on a gate that would refuse them. `ingestBatch` is unreachable from HTTP **by construction**, not by convention: `AppDeps.ingest` is typed to the narrowed `ClientIngest`. **When `POST /v1/sync` is built (Plan 10) it must call `ingestClientBatch`**, or offline-originated events bypass the gate entirely. `apps/server/src/ingest/ingest-service.ts`
- ✅ **A founded workspace arrives usable** — founding appends the default workflow, the four built-in ticket types, and the founder's owner grant, in that order, through the internal door. The founder can therefore act in it the moment it exists — found, create a project, run its roster — rather than facing an empty shell. **It cannot yet hold a TICKET, and the sentence that said so is corrected rather than left standing** (measured by Task 13's route test): `createEngineContext.getTicketTypeDef` answers `null` unconditionally because `proj_ticket_types` does not exist on this branch, so every `ticket.created` in every workspace is refused `ticket.unknown-type` — founded or not, and regardless of the four type definitions the sequence really did append to the ledger. The half this bullet owns is done; the half that makes it observable is Plan 3 Chain B's. *Owner of the remainder: Plan 3 Task 12.* `apps/server/src/workspaces/bootstrap-workspace.ts`, `apps/server/src/engine-context.ts`
- ✅ **Founding cannot be used to seize an existing workspace** — the founding bypass (which must admit `workspace.created` from someone who holds no membership yet, because there is none to hold) is bounded by whether **the workspace** already has a ledger, not by whether the event's entity has a head row. The narrower question is defeated by a founding filed under a fresh entity id against someone else's workspace, which is how a stranger could otherwise have been granted `owner` of a workspace they were never in. **Task 13 closed the other half of the same bound:** that check answers a NON-member, and a member of any role — owner, admin, member or guest — walked straight past it and appended a second, false founding claim to a workspace's own history, which the append-only ledger can never take back. It founded nothing (the grant is stopped one step later, by `workspaceSeq === 1`), but the name shown in the workspace directory is read from exactly those events. The two refusals stay separate sentences because they name different faults: *you are not a member of this workspace* and *this workspace has already been founded*. The INTERNAL door still commits it and still founds nothing — that door is the server's own, and Task 5's bootstrap and Plan 11's seeds use it. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ✅ **Event submission requires a session** — `POST /v1/events` carries `requireSession` and answers a generic `401` before reaching ingest. `apps/server/src/routes/events.ts`
- ⚙️ **Every ticket's resolved audience (`proj_visibility`)** — a projection of `ticket.created`, `ticket.reparented` and `visibility.set`, one row per ticket, carrying both what that ticket itself claimed (`own_mode`/`own_user_ids`) and the audience that actually applies once inherit chains are walked (`resolved_mode`/`resolved_user_ids`/`resolved_from`, never `'inherit'`). An authored mode wins; otherwise the nearest **authored** ancestor's decision is inherited — `resolved_from` names that author, not the immediate parent — and a root that authored nothing falls back to `project`. Re-resolution walks down from whatever changed and **prunes at the first authored descendant**, because nothing above an authored node can change what its subtree sees. The audience is stored as a mode plus an optional list rather than flattened per user, so a membership change never forces re-materialisation. `apps/server/src/projections/proj-visibility.ts`
- ⚙️ **Resolution restores an invariant rather than applying a delta, which is what makes it replay-safe** — every event this projection reads is filed against a **ticket**, so `readLedger`'s canonical `(entity_kind, entity_id, seq)` order is ticket-id order: on a rebuild a parent is routinely created when its own children are already in the table, and a child when its parent is not. The plan text's handler resolved only the new ticket ("a fresh ticket has no children"), which is true of arrival order and false of replay — a grandchild would have been left at the project default while live said otherwise, and `verify-projections` would have reported drift on a table nobody corrupted. So `ticket.created` re-resolves a subtree too, and `visibility.set` is keyed on **`event.entity.id`, never `payload.ticketId`**, so no ticket's stream can write another ticket's row before Task 9 exists to refuse the disagreement. `apps/server/src/projections/proj-visibility.ts`, `apps/server/test/proj-visibility.test.ts`
- ⚙️ **`canSee` — the one visibility decision** — `canSeeResolved(eff, userId, resolved)` is a pure function of the viewer's effective role and the ticket's resolved audience, and it is the whole decision: a **non-member** sees nothing; a **guest** sees only explicit lists that name them (never workspace or project modes, and never through a project attachment — the guest branch does not consult the project set at all); a **member or admin** sees workspace mode always, project mode only from inside the project, explicit mode only when listed; the **owner** sees everything in the workspace. Admins get **no read bypass** — an admin joins a project, an attributed event, and reads afterwards — and the project-admin flag is management, not reading. Nothing here reads a clock, a locale or a random source. `apps/server/src/visibility/can-see.ts`
- ⚙️ **`canSee` reads membership through `resolveEffectiveRole`, and the workspace it asks about is the TICKET's** — never a direct project-scope `SELECT` on `proj_memberships`: a revocation deletes only the workspace-scope row, so a project-scope row **survives** and a bare read returns rows for people no longer in the workspace. `canSee` also takes no `workspaceId` parameter — it resolves the role in the workspace the ticket's own row names, so a role held in one workspace answers nothing about another's ticket. A ticket with no `proj_visibility` row answers `false`, identically to one the viewer may not see. `apps/server/src/visibility/can-see.ts`
- ⚙️ **`listVisibleTickets` — the same table as one SQL predicate** — a viewer's whole slice of a workspace in one round trip rather than N. It is the decision table **said twice**, which is the two-sources-of-truth shape this series keeps catching, so the cost is paid by a test rather than a comment: every viewer class in one fixture is asserted both against its exact expected id set and against `canSee` ticket by ticket, with a non-vacuity assertion so the agreement cannot be agreement about two empty answers. It returns `proj_visibility` rows today — **`proj_tickets` does not exist on this branch** (Plan 3 Task 12 owns it) and no competing definition of it is invented here; the join lands with that table, behind the same WHERE clause and the same tests. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-tickets.ts`
- ✅ **Reads enforce visibility, and an invisible ticket is indistinguishable from one that never existed** — `GET /v1/workspaces/:workspaceId/tickets`, `.../tickets/:ticketId` and `.../tickets/:ticketId/events`, all three behind `requireSession` and all three deferring every audience question to `canSee`. A direct read of a ticket the viewer may not see answers `404 {error:'not_found'}` with the **same status, body and headers** as a read of an id that never existed, so a member probing another project's ids learns nothing; the history door is refused identically, because it is a separate registration that would otherwise have to be refused separately. On the collection the same rule runs the other way: an outsider gets `200 { tickets: [] }`, never a refusal, since an empty/non-empty distinction would be the oracle the 404 discipline removes. The route adds exactly ONE question of its own — is this ticket in the workspace the path names — which is identity, not audience: `canSee` deliberately takes no workspace, so without it a member of two workspaces would read B's ticket through an A-shaped URL. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **The restricted-parent placeholder, and the existence it must NOT invent** — a visible child under a parent it may not see returns `parent: { kind: 'restricted', id }`: existence and id, structurally unable to carry more, so the child never poses as parentless. That is the one deliberate existence leak in the product (spec §6), reachable only through a child the viewer can already see. Its inverse is closed in the same branch: `canSee` answers `false` alike for an invisible ticket and for one with **no row at all**, so collapsing the two would assert the existence of a ticket nothing has evidence for — a `parent_id` naming no ticket returns `parent: null`, the same answer `proj_visibility` gave when it resolved that child as a root. Rows are `proj_visibility`'s and there are **no ticket titles anywhere queryable** on this branch, so the placeholder shipped as existence-versus-the-row rather than existence-versus-title — **and P25 clause 1 inverted that**: the visible parent arm now carries a `title`, so it ships as existence-versus-title, which is the condition this sentence used to record as unmet. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **A served row goes out through an allow-list, because one of `proj_visibility`'s columns is a ticket id** — `resolved_from` names the nearest *authored* ancestor, which is deliberately not the immediate parent, so `SELECT *` would have disclosed the existence of a ticket that is neither visible nor the parent: exactly the leak the placeholder is bounded to avoid. It is cut, along with the explicit audience rosters, in the one mapper both the list and the single read go through; `parent_id` stays, being the same fact as the placeholder itself. The **history endpoint is deliberately not trimmed the same way** — the ancestor ids in a `ticket.created` or `ticket.reparented` payload are provenance, an attributed claim, and spec §6 says provenance is never redacted. Cut derived state; never cut a claim. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **Provenance is never redacted from someone entitled to the state** — ticket history is the ledger, filtered: full envelopes with `actor_id` and both clocks, unchanged even when the actor shares no membership at all with the viewer. Both instants are rendered as absolute ISO 8601 `Z` explicitly rather than left to the serializer, because the session runs in Asia/Kolkata and any other rendering would put an offset label into a field every client parses as a moment. There is deliberately **no second, anonymizing serialization path** — a redacting path that exists is a path something can drift into. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **Rollup honesty — the true total, with the restriction NAMED** — the single-ticket read carries `rollup: { total, restrictedCount } | null`. `total` counts **every** direct child with no visibility filtering whatever, and the part the viewer cannot reach is disclosed beside it rather than subtracted out of it, because a rollup showing only what a viewer can see understates the work and makes a parent look complete when it is not (spec §6). The asymmetry is the design: **`total` is per-parent and `restrictedCount` is per-viewer**, so two people reading the same parent get the same total and different restrictions. There is no third `visibleCount` field: one shipped and was **removed after measurement** — mutating its counter to `total - restrictedCount` killed nothing in the whole server suite, so the invariant defending it was decoration, and the enumeration is cross-checked against `canSee` child by child instead, which is an independent witness rather than arithmetic. The viewer's role is resolved once per **distinct child workspace** — the workspace is the ticket's, never the caller's — rather than once from the first child, and a child in a foreign workspace is counted in the total rather than quietly dropped. **The per-category half is absent rather than zeroed**: "5 of 8 **done**" needs each child's `state_category`, which lives in `proj_tickets` via `proj_rollups`, and neither table exists on this branch (Plan 3 Tasks 11 and 12) — so `open`, `active` and `terminal` are left off the type entirely, since a `0` would be a lie and a `null` still invites a client to render the phrase, while an absent key makes the compiler demand a decision the day the table lands. A childless ticket answers `null`, not a zeroed family, matching what `proj_rollups` will store. `apps/server/src/visibility/rollup.ts`, `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **The sync-down visibility filter — what leaves the server for a device it does not control** — `visibleEventsFor(db, userId, workspaceId, afterSeq)` streams a workspace's ledger past a cursor, filtered against visibility **as of now**, and yields `LedgerEvent | PurgeInstruction | ScopeRevokedInstruction`. An event about a ticket the viewer cannot see now is never yielded — **not the payload and not the id**, because an envelope naming a ticket while withholding its body still discloses that the ticket exists, which is the oracle the read door spent a whole task closing. What a SCOPE REVOCATION inside the window took away comes back as a tombstone instead, since an event that merely stops arriving leaves the data on the device forever: losing a project purges that project's now-invisible tickets, and a revoke-and-re-grant purges what the new role no longer reaches. **A purge is a per-BOUNDARY instruction and only a per-boundary one.** There was a second emitter — a per-EVENT walk that ran on every `visibility.set` and `ticket.reparented`, purging that ticket and every inheriting descendant the viewer cannot see NOW — and a controller ruling struck it. A purge names "cannot see NOW", which is true of a ticket taken away and equally true of one never held; the module cannot tell them apart, because `proj_visibility` keeps no prior audience and neither payload carries a before-image, and re-deriving the projection's `resolve()` rule here against a ledger lookback would be a second definition of visibility that can disagree with the table every other door reads. **What makes an id tolerable is RELATIONSHIP, not size.** The read door's restricted-parent disclosure is acceptable because the viewer already HOLDS the child whose parent id it names; the walk had no such axis — its own test seeded a ticket in a project the viewer was never on and watched the walk purge it — so it was the guest disclosure at a smaller radius, and the cardinality defence for it compared a per-emission quantity against a per-boundary one. It could not be gated on the property it needed, so it is removed rather than approximated. What survives the ruling: a **guest**, and anyone the range put out of the workspace, is never handed a ticket id at all — they are answered at scope level or not at all; at a **scope-revocation boundary** a member or admin still standing inside the workspace may be handed an id they never held, which is the existence-leak spec §6 accepted, bounded by the named scope and fired once for a named person. The cost is recorded rather than hidden and it now falls on guests and members alike: someone un-shared from a ticket by a plain `visibility.set` or `ticket.reparented` keeps the stale copy until a full resync, taken in that direction because an id on a device the server does not control is never retrieved while retained data is corrected by the next bootstrap (owner: Plan 10). `afterSeq` is the **workspace** cursor (`workspace_seq`, coordination §18) and never a per-entity `seq` — every entity restarts its own counter at 1, so ordering the stream by it would bury the entire early history of anything born after the cursor. Membership is asked only through `resolveEffectiveRole`, never off `proj_memberships`, whose project-scope rows survive a revocation by design. The walk's **cycle guard** left with it: the parent graph can contain a loop and nothing on this branch refuses one (case 6.58), and a walk that descended `parent_id` turned that loop into unbounded recursion inside the generator serving every replica — a hang on a request path rather than a wrong answer. The scope scans iterate rows and do not recurse, so this module has no cycle hazard left; refusing a cycle-creating reparent at the door is still Plan 3's. Non-ticket traffic splits by role: workspace, project and membership events go to non-guests; workflow and ticket-type definitions go to everyone, guests included, because a guest cannot render their explicitly-shared ticket's status without the workflow that names its states. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-events-for.ts`
- ✅ **A device whose owner was put out of the workspace is told, and the plan's own code could not tell it** — the plan text opened with `if (eff.workspaceRole === null) return;` and placed the `membership.revoked` purge branch *inside* the loop below it. Those two lines cannot both run for the same person: a revocation deletes the workspace-scope row, so the revoked user's role **is** null and the generator returned before reaching the branch written to serve them. Every ticket on that replica would have stayed there. The discriminator is the **range, not the role** — a stranger's window contains no `membership.revoked` naming them, and someone revoked before the cursor was already told — so a null role plus a revocation in range yields one `{kind:'scope-revoked'}` and stops. It is necessarily scope-level: itemising would mean asking which tickets are invisible to someone invisible to everything, which returns the **whole workspace**, handed as a count to a person now standing outside it. A guest is answered the same way and for the same reason; a member still inside the workspace's accepted existence-leak gets the itemised purges — an arm that shipped executed by **no** test, and whose fixture had to be an **owner** re-granted as a member, since only the owner bypass's loss makes the itemised list a set of genuine retractions rather than a list of ids the recipient never held. On the degenerate shape — revoked and re-granted at `member` inside one window — the recipient has lost nothing at all, because the revocation deletes only the workspace-scope row and the project rows survive it, so every id that arm emits is one they never held; that is stated in the code rather than papered over, and widening the scope instruction to cover it was declined because it would wipe a replica that lost nothing. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-events-for.ts`
- ⚙️ **Which entity kinds may travel is decided by the compiler** — `audienceClassOf` maps every `EntityKind` to one of `ticket · roster · workspace · definition · withheld` through a **bare switch with no `default`**, the idiom `canSeeResolved` established: a kind no `case` names falls out, the function ends without returning, and tsc refuses it with TS2366. Measured rather than asserted — adding a member to `ENTITY_KINDS` raises TS2366 at that function and, on this branch, **nowhere else in the server**, so it is the only place a new kind is forced to declare an audience. Without it a kind added by a later plan would be silently dropped from every replica, or — under a permissive `default` — silently sent to everyone. `invite` is classified `withheld` from **everyone**, owners included: `invite.sent` carries an email address, the owner/admin slice that should carry it is Task 13's, and shipping it to every member meanwhile would disclose the PII that slice exists to protect. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-events-for.ts`
- ⚙️ **`EffectiveRole` is branded, so its provenance is a constraint rather than a convention** — a module-private `unique symbol` that only `effective-role.ts` can write, closing ruling P22. Every safety property proved of `canSeeResolved` rests on its `EffectiveRole` having come from `resolveEffectiveRole`, which is the only thing that makes a null workspace role imply an empty project set; TypeScript is structural, so before the brand any object of the right shape was accepted and a hand-built or stale one would have carried a project set no early return had emptied. It does not make hand-building impossible — two pure-function test helpers legitimately cast — it makes it a **deliberate, greppable assertion at a named site** instead of an accident. `apps/server/src/permissions/effective-role.ts`
- ⚙️ **Scenario seeds built from real events** — `seedMember`, `seedGuest`, `revokeMember`, `seedProject`, `seedTicket`, `setVisibilityEnv` drive the **real projections** with the **real event payloads** rather than writing the projection tables by hand. That is what makes the revoked-member case honest: the surviving project-scope row has to arrive the way a real revocation leaves it, or a test built on it would pass with the safety property deleted. `seedTicket` also appends its `ticket.created` to the **ledger**, because creatorship is read from there and a projection-only fixture would leave every creator lookup answering null. `apps/server/test/helpers/seed.ts`
- ⚙️ **Canonical simple-Kanban workflow template** — the definition a new workspace is founded on, deep-cloned per use so editing a clone can never reach the template. `packages/domain/src/simple-kanban.ts`
- ⚙️ **Canonical errand-with-waits workflow template** — will be pinned by Plan 11's `checkErrandTemplate` oracle (not yet landed on this branch): the frozen tracker's own lane ids, every non-terminal state one hop from `initialStateId` so a seeded record never carries a fabricated intermediate transition, and `require-comment` on every move into `done`. Consumed by identity (never copied) by the workflow builder's template gallery (`packages/app-core/src/workflow-builder/templates.ts`) and, once Plan 11 lands, by the Convenience Corner seed. `packages/domain/src/workflows/errand-with-waits.ts`
- ✅ **Route-level security, proven end to end** — the same three properties Tasks 5, 8 and 9 proved at the gate and the projection are proven again through the real, assembled app over real HTTP: a guest's `POST /v1/events` on a ticket not shared with them is refused `permission.visibility`, each refusal case carrying a **positive control** — the identical caller and route against a ticket the audience check should let through — so the refusal is pinned to the property named rather than to a broken session, route or workspace; a member's direct-id read of another project's ticket answers the same `404 {error:'not_found'}` as one that never existed; and an unauthenticated ingest is `401` before the gate is ever reached. `apps/server/test/routes/security.test.ts`
- ✅ **Founding a workspace through the product — `POST /v1/workspaces`** — one name in, and a workspace that already works comes back: the simple-Kanban default workflow, all four built-in ticket types bound to it, and the founder recorded as owner, appended in that order. The route submits ONE `workspace.created` through the **gated** client door and knows nothing about the sequence behind it — `bootstrapWorkspace` fires from inside the ingest path, so Plan 11's seed CLIs and this door have exactly one founding sequence between them. **The workspace's id is minted server-side and a body that names one is refused**, not ignored: the schema declares `additionalProperties: false`, which is what makes "a client cannot choose the workspace it founds" a refusal rather than an accident of destructuring. A blank or all-spaces name is a 400 that appends nothing. **A sequence that fails partway answers 500 with no id and leaves a half-founded residue that no retry repairs** — the retry mints a fresh ULID and founds a second, complete workspace beside it; the residue holds no grant, so nobody can act in it and the directory never lists it. That behaviour is now measured against a fault injected mid-sequence rather than reasoned from the absence of a `try/catch` (test-cases 6.110, 6.111); the repair path itself is still absent and open at 6.53. Until the owner decision of 2026-08-26 there was no workspace-creation route anywhere in the series: every workspace in every test arrived by hand-appending an envelope. `apps/server/src/routes/workspaces.ts`, `apps/server/src/workspaces/bootstrap-workspace.ts`, `apps/server/test/routes/workspace-bootstrap-failure.test.ts`
- ✅ **The prefixes are ENTERED at both founding forms — up to three letters, upper-cased as the owner types** (owner spec 2026-09-20) — the New-workspace form on `/workspaces` and the New-project form on `/w/<id>` each gained an optional `Prefix` box; `FoundingGateway.foundWorkspace(name, prefix)` carries the first over `POST /v1/workspaces`, and `foundingSeeds` carries the second on the project's founding event. **OPTIONAL in both, and an untouched box sends no key at all** rather than `''`: a workspace or project founded without one still gets a key, because the half is derived from the name — which is why this owner's own three rows already read `CC`, `SO` and `GG` without anybody having typed anything. The forms **upper-case as the owner types**, because `prefixSchema` REFUSES lower case rather than normalising it (the ledger stores a claim as it was made) and the keyboard is the one place that repair belongs.
- ⚙️ **The ticket key — `CCSO-1183`, composed from the ledger and cached NOWHERE** (owner spec 2026-09-20) — **`⚙️` and NOT `✅`, measured at the commit that added it: the key is composed, served, entered and enforced, and until 2026-09-20 NO SCREEN RENDERED IT.** ⚠ **One does now — the ticket sheet's last breadcrumb crumb — and reaching it took a wire change rather than a read.** `GET /v1/me/workspaces` carries the workspace prefix and every project prefix, the composition and its rules are tested end to end, and the display sites are enumerated below rather than wired — so a reader who greps for the key on a board or in the sheet will not find it, and that is a known absence with named owners rather than a broken build. See *"Where the key is shown"* immediately below. A workspace prefix entered at creation, a project prefix entered at creation, and a number scoped **per project**. The key is `workspacePrefix + projectPrefix + '-' + number`. **Every one of the three is a ledger fact and none of them is a stored counter**, which is forced rather than chosen: `events` refuses UPDATE and DELETE (`events_append_only`), so no migration can put a prefix or a number into a founding event, and `tools/verify-projections.ts` diffs every `proj_` table against a rebuilt shadow with `EXCEPT`, so a value a replay could not reproduce is a verifier failure. So a prefix that was **entered** rides the founding payload (`workspace.created`, `project.created`) and a prefix nobody entered is **derived** from the name — initials of the first two words, first two letters of a one-word name — by `derivePrefix`/`resolveProjectPrefixes` (`packages/domain/src/prefix.ts`). The derivation reproduces the three rows the owner named by hand (`Convenience Corner`→`CC`, `Shop operations`→`SO`, `gggg`→`GG`) and so needs no data step on any install. The number is the ticket's **position among its project's creations, ordered by `workspace_seq`** (`read/ticket-numbers.ts`) — a total order with `events_workspace_seq_unique` behind it, assigned under `lockWorkspace`, so two simultaneous creations cannot take one number and there is no allocation to race on. `proj_projects.prefix` is a nullable column written from the payload and from nothing else; NULL means **no prefix was ever entered**, never "no key".
- **Where the key is shown — ENUMERATED, and only the wire half ships** (dispatch §3 item 4, owner: *"it is enough"* — the BARE key is enough at every site, with no project context carried beside it). The owner's ruling decides what to DISPLAY; this list is what makes the decision checkable rather than assumed, and **it is a status list, not a promise**: `-` means implemented, `·` means enumerated and NOT built. **`-` The workspace directory's per-project prefix and the workspace prefix reach the client** (`GET /v1/me/workspaces` → `WorkspaceRef.prefix`, `ProjectRef.prefix`, via `mapWorkspaceRefs`). **`-` The composition itself** (`ticketKey`) and the number's derivation (`read/ticket-numbers.ts`) are callable and tested. **`-` The sheet's breadcrumb crumb** — **BUILT 2026-09-20**, and it was a WIRE change rather than the transcription the dispatch priced: what `#595` shipped is the key's COMPOSITION and the two PREFIXES, and the NUMBER had no route consuming it (measured; `ticketNumber`'s only consumers were its own tests). The detail route composes and serves it (`routes/tickets.ts`, `TicketRead.key`), together with the GRANDPARENT the chain's fourth-crumb-and-above needs (`TicketRead.epic`). Cases `16.69`–`16.71`, and `16.63`–`16.68` for the crumb those two feed. **`·` A ticket URL.** **`·` A search result.** **`·` A mention.** **`·` An MCP tool argument** — no MCP read tool carries a key. **`·` A toast.** **`·` The `Delete` toast's *"deleted · its history is retained"** — ⚠ **THIS SITE NOW EXISTS TO WIRE, corrected at the merge of 2026-09-20: it said *"this site does not exist to wire: `Delete` is HELD"* and that stopped being true when the sheet footer merged (`e9c1a04f`) while this branch ran.** The `Delete` control is in `packages/app-core/src/views/ticket-sheet.tsx` and the soft-delete copy is in the domain. **It is the site the key was invented for**: the prototype's toast reads *"GN-1183 deleted · its history is retained"* and `test-cases/16-remaining-doors.md:522` records that the product has no ticket key to put there. **Owner: whoever next holds the sheet.** **Owner for every `·`: the lane that next touches the sheet**, which is the same lane the soft-delete work is on, precisely so one hand does the header and the key together rather than two lanes editing one component.
- ✅ **The ticket detail route serves the ticket's KEY and its GRANDPARENT** (2026-09-20) — `GET /v1/workspaces/:ws/tickets/:id` carries `key: string | null` and `epic: {kind, id, title} | {kind: 'restricted', id} | null`, which are the two facts the sheet's breadcrumb chain needs and neither of which any read carried. **`key` is COMPOSED on the server, not shipped as three numbers for the client to join**: `ticketKey(workspacePrefix, projectPrefix, number)` — one composer, whose own docblock says *"there is nothing for a caller to forget to pass"* — over `workspaceIdentityFromLedger` (a ledger fold of `workspace.created`/`workspace.renamed`), `projectPrefixesInWorkspace` (stored-or-derived, resolved workspace-wide because the uniqueness rule is) and `ticketNumber` (the ticket's position among its project's creations by `workspace_seq`). **Every absence is `null` and never a refusal** — `read/ticket-numbers.ts`'s ruling, *"a key is not worth refusing a ticket read over"* — so a ticket outside the snapshot still answers a full read. **`epic` is the nearest PROPER ancestor of EPIC rank**, and PROPER is the difference from the board's grouping: `epicAncestorOf` is ancestor-OR-SELF because a lane legitimately holds an epic's own card, while a crumb must not draw an epic one level above itself. ⚠ **The walk uses the route's own `canSee` precedent, one hop at a time, and NOT `routes/list.ts`'s recursive CTE**, because that CTE carries a SQL visibility expression and `routes/tickets.ts`'s own docblock reads *"No SQL here re-states who may see what."* A restricted ancestor answers `{kind: 'restricted', id}` — existence without title, the same shape the parent arm already uses. `AncestorRef` deliberately does NOT carry the `proj_visibility` row the `ParentRef` arm does: `mapParentRef` never reads it, so serving it for the grandparent would put an audience roster on the wire for a reader that does not exist. ⚠ **Offline the replica answers `null` for both**, with owners named, for the reason its `visibility`/`rollup` already are: re-deriving either locally would be a second implementation of a server read. Cases `16.73`, `16.74`. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`, `apps/server/src/read/ancestry.ts`, `packages/app-core/src/view-types.ts`
- ✅ **Project prefixes are unique within a workspace, and the door refuses a duplicate — including one nobody typed** (owner ruling 2026-09-20: *"yes project prefixes are unique per workspace"*) — a second `SO` in one workspace would make `CCSO-1183` name two tickets. Enforced at `checkProjectPrefixUnique` (`permissions/check-permission.ts`, rule `project.prefix-taken`), where the author can be told to choose again. **It checks the DERIVED prefixes too**, which is the load-bearing half: a project that entered nothing still answers to one, and `resolveProjectPrefixes` lets an entered value outrank a derived one — so a column-only check would accept a new `SO` and silently move the older project to `SO2`, changing every key in it after readers had quoted one. Residual scope, pinned rather than discovered: keys are unique **within** a workspace only, and two workspaces may both choose `SO`.
- ✅ **The workspace directory — `GET /v1/me/workspaces`** — the workspaces this person belongs to, with names and roles, read from `proj_memberships` and nothing else. Plan 6's navigation codes against this route and its smoke test MOCKS it; no plan served it, so a signed-in person could found a workspace and then have no way to be told they belong to it. Reading memberships is what makes three things true at once without a special case for any of them: a guest is told where they are a guest (their own membership, not somebody else's data), a revoked membership disappears on the next call, and a workspace whose founding sequence half-landed never appears at all — **the last of those is now observed on a real residue** rather than argued from the absence of a grant, which needed a mid-bootstrap failure to exist before anything could ask. The name is a ledger fold — the latest `workspace.created`/`workspace.renamed` by **workspace_seq**, the workspace's own cursor rather than a per-entity counter or a clock — and collapses into a workspace projection if one ever grows. **Task 16 (2026-09-09) added the counts, and every one of them is the CALLER'S number:** `memberCount` per workspace, and `{ticketCount, blockedCount, archived}` per project. The two ticket counts are visibility-scoped through `visibleTicketsWhere` — the same predicate the board, the list, search, attention and My Work count with — so a member outside a ticket's audience is served a smaller number than an owner, and both are true statements about their reader. This is the other branch of the fork `state-occupancy` takes: that endpoint is admin-only *because* "the honest number is the whole workspace's … so there is no per-viewer variant to serve instead", and a workspace card is served to everybody, so the per-viewer variant is the one that exists here. `blockedCount` imports BOTH halves of that definition from `read/compile-filter.ts` — `BLOCKED_STATES_CTE` and `BLOCKED_MEMBERSHIP`, the latter made exported for this — so the card cannot disagree with the attention strip it links to. Importing the CTE and restating the predicate beside it was the first draft, and it is the half-measure that looks like sharing. `memberCount` counts exactly the roster `GET /v1/workspaces/:id/members` would show this caller — guests included for an admin, withheld from everyone else — sharing `ROSTER_VISIBLE_CLAUSE` with `readMembers` rather than re-expressing it, because a count and a directory are one disclosure. **A guest is served no count at all and the KEYS ARE ABSENT, never 0:** zero is a claim a guest could act on, and the absence is what the card renders as "Restricted". `archived` is not a count and is never withheld — it is a fact about a project whose name the caller is already told. **Task 18 (2026-09-09) added `nameRestrictedProjectCount`, on an owner ruling, and it is the one number here nobody is withheld from.** It counts the projects this caller can see at least one TICKET in (`visibleTicketsWhere`) whose NAME `visibleProjects` withholds — so the card can say *"1 project · name restricted"* truthfully. The escalation that produced it is at `docs/lanes/owner-question-restricted-project-count.md`: the task text asked for a count and `mapWorkspaceRefs`' docblock forbade one in as many words ("the COUNT would itself be the disclosure the rule withholds"), which made it a recorded ruling to reverse rather than an executor's reading. The owner chose **option 2**, verbatim *"go with 2: Count only projects the guest already has sight into"*, and refused **option 3** (count every project the caller cannot name — a plain existence leak). The two agree on any fixture where every project is ticket-visible, so the case seeds a third project the caller can neither name nor see a ticket in. What makes the served number not a disclosure is that a visible ticket card carries its own `projectId`: the reader already holds the ids being counted, so it reveals a NAME restriction and not an existence — the P32 distinction. A **guest is served it**, unlike the two counts above, and copying their guard is the one way to ship this field broken while every member-written fixture stays green. The `mapWorkspaceRefs` comment was AMENDED in the same change set, quoting the sentence it replaces. The SQL half answers with a SET of ids rather than a number, so the name-visibility rule is expressed once (`visibleProjects`) and subtracted in TypeScript; and its tenancy clause is observable at the client door, unlike `visibleProjectCounts`' — this count is over exactly the ids `visibleProjects` did not return, so a foreign workspace-mode ticket lands straight in it. `apps/server/src/routes/workspaces.ts`, `apps/server/src/workspaces/workspace-name.ts`, `apps/server/src/read/workspace-counts.ts`, `apps/server/src/read/membership-roster.ts`
- ✅ **Invitations — an address is a claim, not an identity** — an admin invites a person by address (email or phone), never by user id, so somebody who has never signed in can be brought in: `invite.sent` is the attributed claim, the link's token is server-owned and hashed (never in the ledger — reading a workspace's history confers nothing), and acceptance on landing binds the invitation and appends the membership it promised. `invite.sent`/`invite.revoked` are admin-only; `invite.accepted` and (Task 17) `invite.declined` are refused from every client door — acceptance without proof would let anyone who can read an invite id bind it to themselves, and a decline without proof would let them destroy it under the invited person's name. A FOURTH type since Task 17, `invite.declined` carries `{inviteId}` alone and deliberately no `userId`: declining makes no binding and nothing follows it, so the actor on the envelope is the only record needed, and the vocabulary's invariant holds — no payload here lets an address and a user id appear together. `proj_invites.status` gained `declined` and stayed a one-way door: all three terminal transitions carry `AND status = 'pending'`, so the first terminal claim in ledger order stands. An invitation cannot confer ownership, a guest invitation cannot carry projects, a promised project must exist in the inviting workspace or issuance is refused before any credential is minted, and an existing member's role is never changed by a link somebody forwarded — nor does an existing guest pick up a project row from a member invitation they accept, which is the same guest rule applied to the acceptor rather than to the invitation. `apps/server/src/routes/invites.ts`, `packages/domain/src/events/invites.ts`
- ✅ **The manual and FAQ, inside the product** — reachable at `/help` on the web, from a header button on the phone's board, and from a toggle on the desktop shell **including its signed-out branch**. Needs no session, no network and no workspace: the content is bundled, which is the point, since the FAQ's answers about signing in and about being offline are worth least behind a sign-in or over a network. Searchable — conjunctive, title-weighted, and it says *which kind* of nothing it found — and this is currently the only search a web user can reach at all. Restyled onto the shared radius/shadow token system (2026-09-15) — no content change. **One structural element was added later the same day** (`.gn-help__intro`, a presentational wrapper grouping the `h1` with the search box), because the reading column became a flex container and the reference sets those two closer to each other than to anything around them — a margin on the `h1` would have added to the column's `gap` instead of replacing it. Nothing an assistive technology reads changes: the `h1` and `label` keep their own roles. **STYLING, 2026-09-15 (owner, against a newer desktop-prototype revision): `/help` gained its own `<h1>Help</h1>` and its table of contents was flattened.** The page previously rendered NO `h1` at all — measured live, not inferred — so the first heading was an `h2` from the document body and the hierarchy started at level 2; the title lives in `HelpBrowser` rather than in `apps/web/app/help/page.tsx` for the reason that component exists, since web and desktop both render it and a title owned by one shell is a title the other lacks. It is an ADDITION under the standing copy rule ("reject anything that loses information or an aria-label; keep shipped wording where it's merely different; adopt the additions" — owner, 2026-09-08), there being no shipped wording to replace. `.gn-help__toc` — the flat wrapped row of section links — **no longer exists: the topic sidebar below replaced it later the same day.** It had just been flattened out of a raised card (`surfaceRaised` + `shadowRaised` + card radius) onto the reference's bare `gap: 7px 20px` row, a change that was right about the older reference and is now moot; recorded because the sidebar is the same index, grouped, always visible and marking where the reader is, and two indexes on one page would be one too many. Nothing was lost with it — every anchor it pointed at is still rendered, still reachable from the sidebar, and still reachable from an external deep link. **TWO REPLACEMENTS WERE REJECTED, not overlooked:** the prototype's short `Manual`/`FAQ` tab labels (shipped: the full document titles) and its `e.g. contested claim` search placeholder (shipped: `due date, passkey, offline…`) are both wording that is merely different, which the same copy rule keeps shipped — and both prototype strings carry less information than the text they would replace. **A tracking correction found in the same pass:** the title's tracking shipped as `-0.025em`, which measures **−0.85px** at 34px against the reference's **−0.4px**; both `.gn-help__title` and `/account`'s `.gn-account .gn-page-title` are now `-0.0118em` (−0.4px ÷ 34px), measured live at −0.4012px. `packages/app-core/src/help/`, `apps/web/app/help/`, `apps/mobile/src/screens/help-screen.tsx`, `packages/ui/src/ui.css`, `packages/app-core/test/help/help-browser.test.tsx`
  - ✅ **A topic sidebar that navigates the whole document, not one section of it** (owner, 2026-09-15, same newer prototype revision) — a 274px `aside` holding the document tabs, a `Topics` label and five collapsible manual groups with counts, plus a `MANUAL · GETTING STARTED` eyebrow over each section and a `Next` pager at its foot. **The shipped content model is `document → section → entry` with no group layer**, so the groups are DECLARED in `packages/app-core/src/help/groups.ts` — option (b) of the four the design note weighed, chosen over reshaping `docs/manual.md` (options (a)/(d)) because the group NAMES are an invention pending the owner's review and provisional copy does not belong in the place hardest to revise; (d) is still the destination once they settle. The five are **Getting started (4), Workspaces and people (2), Working with jobs (5), Permissions and setup (3), Devices and details (2)**, written in the product's own vocabulary — it says *jobs*, never "tickets" — and kept in one array so all five can be rewritten in a single edit. **The FAQ declares no groups and renders flat, which is a decision rather than an omission:** its 13 sections are already topic names ("Signing in", "Privacy"), so a layer over them would restate the first index; the prototype groups ACROSS both documents and filters to the open tab, which only works because its FAQ has five sections. **ONE INDEX DOES SURVIVE ON THE FAQ TAB, and it is not this change's to remove:** `docs/faq.md:8` opens with its own `**Jump to:** …` line naming all 13 sections, which renders above the body beside a sidebar listing the same 13. It is shipped prose in the source document, so the copy rule keeps it; removing it means editing `docs/faq.md`, which is the owner's call. Named because the sidebar's own justification for replacing `.gn-help__toc` was that two indexes on one page are one too many — and on the FAQ tab there are still two. **ONE ITEM OF THE PROTOTYPE'S DESIGN IS DECLINED: one section rendered at a time.** The copy rule opens with *"reject anything that loses information"*, and drawing one section of 16 would cost browser find across all **224** entries, every search result's `#document-entry` link, and every external deep link — none of which has a replacement. The sidebar is navigation over the full document instead, so the information architecture comes over at none of that cost. **Groups default OPEN and the collapse does not persist** — collapsed-by-default would put every section behind a click and make this a worse index than the flat row it replaced. **The active row follows the READER**, on the owner's instruction — *"since its a single page document, highlight the section that is currently displayed in the reading pane"*. An `IntersectionObserver` scroll spy over the rendered sections, with `rootMargin: '-15% 0px -70% 0px'` to narrow the root to a band across the upper quarter of the viewport: "anywhere on screen" is three sections at once on a wide monitor, and the reading position is the question being asked. Where two sections share the band the EARLIEST in document order wins, so the highlight advances once the outgoing section's tail leaves the band rather than flickering back and forth across every boundary. **The URL fragment is kept as the second half, not replaced:** the spy reports nothing until its first callback lands a frame after mount, and nothing at all where `IntersectionObserver` is absent, so a deep link into `#manual-10-…` marks the right row on the FIRST paint and the fragment path is the only one jsdom can exercise. They agree once both have an answer, since the browser scrolls a fragment target into view. A search result names an ENTRY, so the fragment path resolves entry → owning section. **Two defects in the spy were found by scrolling a real browser, not by reasoning:** its first cut kept the last answer when the band emptied, which meant NO row was marked at the top of the document (the `h1`, search box and intro fill the band) and a STALE row stayed marked after scrolling back up — a confident wrong answer, worse than none. An empty band now falls back to geometry: the last section whose top has passed the viewport top, else the first section. Measured after the fix at five scroll positions: exactly one row marked at every one, the first section at the top, and the first section again on return. Marked with `aria-current='location'` and not `'page'` — the target is a place within this page, which is the distinction ARIA draws, and `'page'` would tell a screen reader the reader had navigated away.
  - ✅ **Only the active row is filled; every other row is plain text** (owner, 2026-09-15, from a screenshot of the reference's rendered sidebar plus its own style builder) — and this corrected a real defect rather than restyling a working thing. Every row used to be `--gn-text-accent` at 1rem with a hover underline, i.e. every row painted as a link: twenty-one accent-blue rows compete for attention and none wins, so the ONE row meaning "you are here" had nothing left but a translucent wash to distinguish it. Inactive rows are now `--gn-text-muted` at 15px/400 with no decoration at all and a `--gn-fill-quaternary` hover; the active row is `--gn-accent` + `--gn-accent-text` at 600, which is the reference's own `--accent`/`--onColor` in this repo's roles and a DECLARED pair (`{ fg: 'accentText', bg: 'accent', minimum: 4.5 }`, `tokens.ts:721`) already used by `.gn-avatar--tint-0`. The previous `--gn-text-accent` on `--gn-fill-tertiary` was the OPPOSITE composition — accent-hued text over a translucent fill — which is exactly the distinction Round 2d split those two role names apart to keep. Contrast improved as a side effect: the active row now measures **5.26:1 light / 7.48:1 dark** against the old wash's 4.54:1. **`box-shadow: var(--gn-shadow-sunken)` is the one addition beyond the reference's string**, because the owner asked for a *depressed* style: under `neo` that token is a real inset shadow (verified present), under `hig`/`minimal` it resolves to `none` (verified), so those two keep the fill and the weight — which carry the state on their own, unlike the shadow-only cues this file has had to fix twice. Row height 34px and font-size 15px are the reference's, both adopted over an earlier 24px/1rem: **that 1rem was reasoned from a wrong premise** — a comment claiming a "16px floor for body text on a phone" — when the floor this repo actually enforces is 12px, derived at length in `apps/web/e2e-viewport/type-scale.ts`. The group toggle moved to 15px/600 with it, so the reference's weight-based hierarchy replaces a size-based one. **The document switch is the reference's own, on an owner ruling that reversed an earlier one.** It shipped first as two bordered buttons carrying the documents' full titles, stacked into a column because "Gisik Nokma — instruction manual" is 31 characters and will not sit beside its sibling in 274px — the copy rule kept the wording and the layout gave way. The owner then lifted that ruling for this control in as many words: *"forget the ruling on the information losing. make the manual/faq switch like in the attached file"*. So the labels are the reference's `Manual` and `FAQ` (from its own source, `helpTabs: [{ k: "manual", l: "Manual" }, { k: "faq", l: "FAQ" }]`), which also match the shipped search label's phrasing *"Search the manual and FAQ"* — and with two short words the reference's side-by-side pair fits. **Rebuilt on the shared `SegmentedControl` rather than a local rule**, because the reference's treatment IS that component's: measured against its inline styles, tray `padding: 2px` / `gap: 2px` exact, `flex: 1` segments at 118px in a 242px tray, 15.2px against its 15px, weights 600/500 exact. ONE deliberate divergence — segment height 44px against its 36px, because `ui.css` records that 40px reddened the tap-target ratchet on `/sign-in` the moment anything first rendered the component. This retires a whole local identity: `/account`'s Style and Theme controls are already `SegmentedControl`, and a fifth hand-rolled segmented row on this screen was the thing to avoid. The `aria-label` carries the string the old `<nav>` wrapper used, so no accessible name is lost; the wrapper itself is gone deliberately, since a `tablist` is a control and wrapping one in `<nav>` announced a region that was never navigation. **`.gn-help__tabs` is deleted and `.gn-help__tab` is renamed `.gn-help__toggle`** — it had one remaining user, `apps/desktop/src/app.tsx`'s "Manual & FAQ" / "Back" button, which was never a tab. **That deletion also broke a check in a way nothing would have reported:** `.gn-help__tabs` flex-direction was one of two discriminators `apps/web/e2e-viewport/responsive.spec.ts` reads to prove the 40rem breakpoint fired, and the spec guards its assertion with `if (… !== null)`, so an absent selector turns a firing check into a SKIPPED one. The discriminator moved to `.gn-help__h2`'s font-size, the other declaration already in that media query. ONE CONSEQUENCE named rather than left to be noticed: the documents' full titles no longer appear anywhere on the screen, though they are still on the `HelpDocument` for anything that wants them. **Two measured cross-app facts, neither inferred:** `.gn-help` changed meaning — it used to BE the 72ch column and is now the two-column shell, with `.gn-help__column` carrying the 72ch — and `apps/desktop` renders the same component while loading only `ui.css`, which has no `box-sizing` reset, so the sidebar measured **306px there against 274px on web** until `box-sizing: border-box` was stated on the rule. Also fixed in passing and already broken: the help tab button rendered the UA's `2px outset rgb(0, 0, 0)` because the rule never reset `border` and nothing resets `button` globally — the fix travelled with the rule into `.gn-help__toggle`, verified live there at `border: 0px none`. Verified live in both apps, across all six style×theme combinations (the active row's worst contrast is **4.54:1** under hig/minimal light, against a 4.5 floor) and at a real 390×844 (stacked, no horizontal overflow, no tap target under 24px). Cases 15.19–15.24. `packages/app-core/src/help/groups.ts`, `packages/app-core/src/help/help-browser.tsx`, `packages/ui/src/ui.css`, `packages/app-core/test/help/groups.test.ts`, `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-15-help-group-layer-note.md`
  - ⚙️ **The desktop window installs the design tokens it draws with** — `ui.css` references the tokens and defines none of them (321 `var(--gn-…)` usages, zero declarations), so until pre-launch item 6 the desktop shell rendered its help browser on browser defaults: only `apps/web`'s layout ever emitted `themeStylesheet()`. Confirmed in the shipped artifact — the built desktop CSS carried 24 `var(--gn-…)` usages and no definitions. Neither existing guard could see it, because both are claims about the CSS text rather than about any app's entry point actually installing a stylesheet. Light plus dark, with the OS-preference default; no theme toggle here yet, so the web shell's stored-choice script is deliberately not ported. **And it was shipping with no TYPEFACE either, found 2026-09-15 while porting the prototype's fonts — the same shape, one property along:** `ui.css` declares no `font-family` (a base typeface is not a BEM component's to set) and `index.html` carries no `<style>`, so the only stack the product named lived in `apps/web/app/globals.css`, which this app never loads, and every glyph in the window was the browser's default serif while `apps/web` rendered the same `HelpBrowser` in the system sans. `installTheme` now appends a SECOND element, `data-gn-base`, carrying `body { font-family: var(--gn-font-sans); -webkit-font-smoothing: antialiased }` — separate from `data-gn-tokens` deliberately, because that element is `themeStylesheet()` verbatim and `theme.test.ts` asserts the equality, which is what makes "the shared origin arrived unedited" checkable at all. The stack is not written there: it reaches the rule through `fontVariableName('sans')`, so `tokens.ts` stays the only place a typeface is spelled out. **Named rather than quietly fixed: this window still sets no `background` or `color` on `body`** (`globals.css` does, for web) — a real remaining absence, left because it is a colour question with a contrast argument attached and that change was about typefaces. Cases 15.30, 15.32. `apps/desktop/src/theme.ts`, `apps/desktop/src/main.tsx`
- ⚙️ **One parse, two renderers, three apps** — `docs/manual.md` and `docs/faq.md` are parsed ONCE at build time into a closed block model (`scripts/help-content.mjs` → `content.generated.ts`). React Native cannot render HTML, so the web's renderer can never be the phone's; parsing once is what stops three renderers becoming three parsers that disagree about one sentence. Web and desktop share a single React DOM component; mobile has its own React Native screen over the same parsed object. **`pnpm check:help` refuses a generated copy that disagrees with its source**, and the parser self-tests against planted defects before certifying anything — a generated artifact with no freshness gate goes stale silently, and this one would show users an old manual with no symptom. `scripts/help-content.mjs`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`
- ✅ **A 404 that says which kind of not-found it cannot rule out** — three causes named together, and an explicit refusal to guess between them, because the API answers the same 404 for "does not exist" and "not yours" so that nobody can probe for which records exist. The page explains that reasoning rather than apologising for it. **To the refreshed export (Step 2c-2, 2026-09-18): a 72px `aria-hidden` icon tile above the heading, a THIRD action link to the manual (`/help`, labelled `Search the manual` — the export's own order is workspaces / manual / inbox, and this page's two existing links already agreed with it), the export's heading scale (34px/41px/700/-0.4px) and a centred 520px column in place of the shell's 80rem. The COPY IS DELIBERATELY NOT PORTED** — the export says "This page isn't here" / "Nothing was deleted." and the audit measured both at zero product hits; adopting them would answer the question this page exists to refuse. The export is thinner than this page here, not ahead of it, so nothing was removed to make the two look alike. Cases 13.686–13.689. `apps/web/app/not-found.tsx`, `apps/web/app/globals.css`, `apps/web/test/not-found-and-legal.test.tsx`
- ✅ **Privacy policy and terms pages** — reachable at `/privacy` and `/terms`, written from what the product technically does (data in India, an append-only history, no passwords, one outbound email) with every party-specific fact left as a bracketed placeholder. **Both are unreviewed boilerplate and say so in a code comment, never on the rendered page** — a test asserts the marker is in the source AND absent from the render. The terms carry the four things the product does not promise, asserted so a later cleanup cannot quietly drop them. **To the refreshed export (Step 2c-2, 2026-09-18): a top bar, a segmented Privacy/Terms pair, an anchor row built from the page's own headings, and the export's heading scale.** The bar is the export's `isLegal` (`desktop.dc.html@6d7b67c0a52c:1722-1729`) — brand tile, `Gisik Nokma` wordmark, and a `Sign in` link when anonymous; signed in, it mounts the product's OWN `AccountMenu` + `InboxNavLink` corner rather than inventing a second nav. It renders through the product's `.gn-nav` chrome. The anchor row and the `<h2>`s render from ONE array per page with each anchor id computed from its heading, so the row cannot describe a page other than the one beneath it. **`BackButton` is REPLACED, and that is a real loss stated rather than absorbed:** the export draws no back affordance anywhere in `:1720-1755`, so the control (owner feedback, 2026-09-08 — both pages linked only to EACH OTHER) was removed with it; its no-history branch went to `/sign-in` and the bar's `Sign in` link goes to the same route, so a reader who landed with no history still has a destination, but **the one-press return for a reader who has history is gone**. `apps/web/src/back-button.tsx` and its `.gn-legal__back` rule were DELETED with it, since nothing else mounted them; the reasoning they carried lives in `apps/web/src/legal-nav.tsx` and in the catalogue. Cases 13.322–13.323 SUPERSEDED; cases 13.682–13.685. `apps/web/app/privacy/page.tsx`, `apps/web/app/terms/page.tsx`, `apps/web/src/legal-nav.tsx`, `apps/web/src/legal-chrome.tsx`, `apps/web/app/globals.css`
- ⚙️ **HTTPS is enforced by the layer that owns each half, and HSTS by this app** — the HTTP→HTTPS redirect belongs to the platform router (`deploy/do/app.yaml`, DigitalOcean App Platform), so no in-app redirect exists and writing one would be unreachable code; the `Secure` cookie flag is derived by better-auth from `BETTER_AUTH_URL`'s protocol; and `Strict-Transport-Security` — which no layer supplied — is sent by BOTH hosts, since HSTS is scoped per host and the API sending it says nothing about the web shell. `max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains`, and `preload` is deliberately absent and asserted absent. Refuses to emit over plain http (RFC 6797 §7.2), so local dev is untouched. `apps/server/src/http/hsts.ts`, `apps/web/next.config.ts`
- ⚙️ **The env-documentation gate covers every shipping client, not just the web one** — `check:env` walks `apps/mobile` and `apps/desktop` as well, and detects Vite's `import.meta.env` alongside `process.env`. Both were blind spots that let four client-inlined build-time variables ship undocumented while the gate reported ok; one of them (`EXPO_PUBLIC_DEV_TOKEN`) is a real bearer token that a bundler substitutes into the artifact. `scripts/check-env-example.mjs`, `.env.example`
- ✅ **An invitation link lapses and is spent — spent by ONE person, decided by the database** — every minted token carries a real `expires_at` (14 days, one constant, a default under uncertainty the owner may replace) and is consumed at acceptance by a single conditional statement, `DELETE ... WHERE token_hash = $1 AND expires_at > now() RETURNING invite_id`. One statement is one transaction, so at most one presentation of a token can get a row back: concurrent presentations queue on the row lock and match nothing. **That is what makes the link single-use, and the `proj_invites.status` check is not** — a read cannot stop two people presenting the same link at once, and until this was fixed three parallel acceptances all answered 200 and all joined, in 24 of 25 trials, while the projection's own `pending` guard dropped the surplus `invite.accepted` and hid it (test-case 6.129). The status check keeps its own job: saying whether the invitation still opens anything at all, so an unknown, lapsed, revoked, already-accepted, or hand-restored token row opens nothing. Acceptance CONSUMES before it appends — a crash between leaves a pending invitation with no credential, which the pending list already surfaces as `expiresAt: null` for the admin to withdraw and reissue. Unknown, revoked, spent and lapsed links answer one identical 404 body on both token-addressed routes, because a dead link that explains why it is dead is an oracle. **A revocation submitted while an acceptance is in flight still does not stop the membership** — recorded and owned at 6.130, not claimed closed. `apps/server/src/routes/invites.ts`
- ⚙️ **The credential store that is not a projection (`invite_tokens`)** — one migration creates two tables of different natures. `proj_invites` rebuilds from the ledger; `invite_tokens` never can, because the token is deliberately absent from the ledger. It is excluded from `projInvites.tables` so `reset()`, `rebuildProjection` and `verify-projections` cannot reach it — listing it would destroy every live invitation link in the product on the next rebuild, and nothing could put them back. `apps/server/migrations/1787961600000_proj-invites.sql`, `apps/server/src/projections/proj-invites.ts`
- ✅ **An invitation's address reaches owners and admins and nobody else** — `invite` is the first entity kind withheld from plain members in sync-down: the payload carries an address somebody gave in confidence, a member has no screen that needs it, and an event on a replica is on a device the server does not control. The same rule governs the read path, so `GET /v1/workspaces/:workspaceId/invites` — the pending list, which never re-serves a token — refuses a plain member `permission.role`. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-events-for.ts`, `apps/server/src/routes/invites.ts`
- ✅ **The inviter's identity reaches an AUTHENTICATED recipient and NEVER the token page** (owner ruling 2026-09-19 late; `owner-holds.md:74`) — the `/workspaces` invitation banner names who asked: *"Rukhang invited you to Convenience Corner as a member."* That is the prototype's own sentence (`desktop.dc.html:216` — *"Anjali invited you to Garo Hills Logistics as a member."*, with its Open/Decline pair), and it landed with the caller-scoped invitations read in `#400` (`d3d0abdd`), where `invitedByName` comes from a `LEFT JOIN "user"` on `proj_invites.invited_by` and answers `null` — never an invented `'Unknown'` — when the inviter's account has since gone. **The ruling's other half is the ABSENCE, and that is the half nothing pinned:** the session-less token page names no inviter **in any branch, the signed-in one included**, because its audience is whoever holds the URL, which `routes/invites.ts`'s own docblock records *"may have been forwarded"* — the same reason that route withholds the invited address. Case `16.44`. ⚠ **This item was carried as an outstanding BUILD, and measured on 2026-09-20 it was ALREADY BUILT and tested** — the record is corrected rather than left for the next session to implement twice; the lesson is the one the `isInvite` decline row already leaves (`owner-holds.md`: a row written from a recommendation rather than a measurement). `apps/web/app/workspaces/page.tsx`, `apps/web/test/workspaces-invitation-banner.test.tsx`, `apps/web/app/invite/[token]/page.tsx`, `apps/web/test/invite-page.test.tsx`
- ⚙️ **The gate names the Node it ran under** — `engines` is declared in the root manifest alone, so pnpm's `WARN Unsupported engine` never fires for a per-package script and an absent warning proved nothing (measured under Node v24: silent). A test inside the run asserts `process.version` against the root manifest's own range, parsed rather than restated, and its failure message names the Node. `apps/server/test/node-version.test.ts`
- ✅ **The invitation token does not reach the process log** — the token travels in the URL path, because `GET /v1/invites/:token` is the pinned client contract, and Fastify's default `info` request line carries `req.url` verbatim (measured). A `req` serializer redacts that one segment and leaves every other path alone, so log access does not become workspace access. `apps/server/src/app.ts`, `apps/server/src/routes/invites.ts`
- ✅ **A dead invitation link stops costing anything — the credential store is collected, not merely ignored** — a revoked or lapsed invitation's `invite_tokens` row opens nothing, but nothing was ever removing it either: acceptance deletes its own row and a dead link being presented does not clear it, because `openableInvite` refuses on status or expiry BEFORE the consume statement is reached. So the table was monotonic, holding password-equivalent hashes for credentials that had been withdrawn. `sweepInviteTokens` collects both kinds, and the revoked kind is the one that matters most: a withdrawal can land at any moment before natural expiry, so an expiry-only sweep would keep a cancelled workspace's credential for the rest of its fortnight. **One predicate string decides what a dry run reports AND what a live run deletes**, because two separately maintained `WHERE` clauses drift invisibly — both keep working, they just stop describing each other. The fragment is PARENTHESISED, because its top level is an `OR` and `AND` binds tighter: unwrapped, the first caller to append `AND workspace_id = $1` would silently sweep every lapsed token in every workspace, so "one predicate, safe in both callers" holds only under composition. Safe at any moment, safe twice; the scheduling surface is deliberately not here. `apps/server/src/tools/sweep-invite-tokens.ts`, `apps/server/test/tools/sweep-invite-tokens.test.ts`
- ✅ **Per-user data does not outlive the relationship that justified it** — `user_preferences` has no foreign keys and preferences are not events, so no purge tombstone reaches the table: a revoked member's arrangement survived forever, unreachable behind the door's 404 and collected by nothing. Storage was never the point; the statement was. `sweepUserPreferences` keeps such a row for one named window and then removes it, and the two hard parts are stated rather than assumed. **Who is a member is resolved through `resolveEffectiveRole` and never by reading `proj_memberships` directly** (ruling P22) — a revocation deletes only the workspace-scope row, so a direct read would report a departed person as current and keep their data forever, the very thing being fixed. The rule is pinned by the one fixture in which the two readers disagree — a revoked person whose PROJECT-scope roster row is still standing — because in every state built from workspace-scope facts alone the forbidden read returns the same answer and the rule tests as vacuous. **The DELETE re-states the membership condition rather than trusting the decision made three round-trips earlier**: the steps are separate statements with no enclosing transaction, and `user_preferences` is authored and rebuilt by nothing, so a grant committing mid-sweep would otherwise destroy a now-current member's row unrecoverably. **When they stopped being a member is read from the LEDGER**, because the revocation deletes the row that would otherwise have held the instant; a non-member with no `membership.revoked` event has no window start, so the sweep fails closed and NAMES the row in its report rather than skipping it silently. The window is one constant, disclosed as a default under uncertainty for the product owner to confirm or replace, and `now` is injected so the boundary is testable without sleeping through it. `apps/server/src/tools/sweep-user-preferences.ts`, `apps/server/test/tools/sweep-user-preferences.test.ts`

## 7. API, realtime & search

*(**Plan 5, complete: Tasks 1–16.** Task 16 is this plan's catalogue task and has now run, so this
note is the replacement it owed rather than the placeholder that stood here. Every `/v1` surface Plan
5 promised is present: the board, the project list, the builder read surface and the one write that
belongs with it (the bulk workflow rebind), search, My Work, the attention strip, view preferences,
and the WebSocket route that mounts the hub. Plan 5 Task 1 (`docToPlainText`) keeps its bullet in §3,
beside the ledger material it belongs to, on the precedent §3 already records for Plan 6's and Plan
10's pulled-forward bullets; its test cases are **7.1, 7.2 and 7.159** in
`test-cases/07-api-realtime-search.md`. §8, §10 and §11 stay absent because the coordination doc
assigns `Features.md` sections one per implementation plan, so numbering is claimed from that table
rather than taken next-free.)*

*What this surface does **not** do — enumerated, because a partial surface that only calls itself
partial tells a reader nothing about which half they have. Every item below is a decision with a
reason on record, not an oversight:*

- *Search answers the **top 50, unpaginated**, ordered by `ts_rank_cd` then ticket id, and its
  configuration is `FTS_CONFIG = 'simple'` — **nothing is stemmed, in any language**. Both were
  settled deliberately rather than deferred: stemming one language while not stemming the others
  would rank dishonestly for an audience that writes in more than one. Pagination stays additive
  (`hits` is already an array) and a language-aware configuration is a reindex, not a shape change.*
- *My Work is **per workspace** (`?workspaceId=`). There is no cross-workspace aggregation on the
  server; a client that wants one loops. A server-side join would need cross-workspace session
  scoping this series has not defined, and inventing it inside a view endpoint is how scoping rules
  end up owned by nobody.*
- *View preferences are **full-replace `PUT`** per (user, workspace). There is no per-key `PATCH` and
