- ⚙️ **The dev stack serves BUILT images, rebuilt on every merge to `main`** — `pnpm dev:stack` brings it
  up, `pnpm dev:stack:rebuild` rebuilds and brings it up. The stack is `docker-compose.dev.yml` **alone**:
  `web` on :3001, `server` on :3000, real Postgres on 54330, MinIO, Mailpit. **There is no bind-mounted
  source and no watcher**, so the served code equals `main` exactly as of the last build. It is run from
  `/Users/kiro/my_projects/gisik-nokma` — the MAIN folder — because the images bake `SERVER_ORIGIN` and
  `NEXT_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL` as BUILD args and `next build` serialises them into `.next`: **an image is a
  snapshot of the tree it was built from.**
  **The rebuild rule is the load-bearing part, and its failure shape is this repo's recorded
  stale-serving class.** Merge to `main` and skip `pnpm dev:stack:rebuild`, and the app keeps serving the
  PREVIOUS build while `/healthz` answers 200, both containers are `Up`, and `:3001` returns its usual
  307 — every signal green, the code older than `main`. With the watchers gone the rebuild is the only
  thing that closes it, and no health check can tell you. `--env-file .env` (which both scripts pass) is
  what converts a MISSING `.env` from a silent empty substitution — server healthy, **Google sign-in
  OFF** — into a hard stop.
  **HOT-RELOAD MODE WAS RETIRED HERE ON 2026-09-19** by owner instruction: *"no more hot reloads. rebuilt
  once they hit the main, is fine. that means the servers are to be run from the main folder instead of
  from the executor's branch."* No executor's branch is ever live any more, which also retires the
  ":3001 belongs to executor 1" rule that existed only because the container mounted that worktree.
  `docker-compose.hot.yml` is **kept and deliberately no longer composed by anything**, because a deleted
  file takes its reasons with it — the rename-over save shape not crossing the Docker Desktop mount;
  `tsx watch` losing its watch **permanently** on the first such save; `node --watch --import tsx` measured
  failing even on a write made INSIDE the container; busybox `kill -0` succeeding on an exited-but-unreaped
  child, so a liveness supervisor cannot fire; `compose` v2 shell-word-splitting a string-form `command`.
  **The incident that motivated hot mode is why the rebuild rule is written in this register**: the running
  server image was built **2026-09-09T12:32Z** while the first `closed` fold reached
  `apps/server/src/read/ticket-cards.ts` on **2026-09-17**, so the owner's Backlog tab read cards with no
  `closed` key from a server eight days behind the client, and the mapper refused it
  (`list.groups[0].cards[0].closed: expected an object`). The mapper's refusal stays strict — a `closed`
  that defaults to `null` when absent is what hid case 13.603 the first time.
  `docker-compose.dev.yml`, `docker-compose.hot.yml` (retired; kept for its measurements), `package.json`,
  `apps/server/Dockerfile`, `apps/web/Dockerfile` (both keep `dev` stages that no shipped command targets
  any more, and both stage comments now say so; **their `dev` stage's position is still load-bearing** —
  see the rebuild rule), `docs/runbook.md`,
  `test-cases/13-owner-requests-and-backlog.md` (**13.946** is the replacement case; hot mode's witness
  record **13.650–13.657** is superseded in place rather than deleted),
  `docs/lanes/executor-state.md`
- ✅ **Deployed-stack E2E — Playwright against the running compose stack, and the sign-in defect it
  found** — three specs drive the REAL stack (a Next container proxying to a real API server, real
  Postgres, real Mailpit, real MinIO): sign-in through the shipped form with the magic link fetched out of
  the test inbox, the seeded Convenience Corner board rendered from real projections, `POST /v1/events`
  through the web container's proxy carrying the session cookie the browser earned, and a guest seeing the
  workspace but NOT its projects — ruling P38's partial workspace, seen from a browser for the first time.
  Run with `pnpm run test:e2e:stack` against a stack that is already up. It lives under a **second**
  Playwright config (`apps/web/playwright.stack.config.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/`) with **no `webServer`**,
  because the existing mock-only smoke's config starts `pnpm dev` on 3001 with `reuseExistingServer` — so
  real-stack specs under it would either collide with the compose web container or silently adopt a stray
  dev server and report deployment coverage of a deployment they never touched. It is off the gate
  **structurally**: `test:e2e:stack` is a script name turbo defines no task for (measured before and
  after: 16 tasks, 10 `#test`, zero e2e), which matters because playwright declares no install script, so
  the browser binary is an explicit CI step and nowhere else. `global-setup.ts` REFUSES the run unless what
  is listening is really the stack — a proxied `/v1` request must be answered by the API server, with the
  server's own error body, inside a time bound — and that assertion has a measured negative control rather
  than an argued one: an image built without `SERVER_ORIGIN` (what `up -d` reuses without `--build`)
  proxies `/v1/*` at itself and answers **500 after 30 s**, while `docker compose ps` reports every
  container healthy in both states. The Mailpit helper purges the inbox immediately before each send and
  then requires **exactly one** message, because `messages[0]` under an unknown sort order can return an
  already-spent link and following one does not fail loudly — the earlier session cookie is still in the
  context. **Its seed step is SHAPE-AWARE and has to be — there are two images this suite runs against
  and only one of them has a `dist`** (Step 5b, 2026-09-18). The prod-parity image carries `/app/dist`
  and no `src`, so `node dist/seed/cli-seed-convenience-corner.js` is right there; the hot image
  bind-mounts `apps/server/src` at `/repo/apps/server/src`, has WORKDIR `/repo`, and has **no `dist`
  anywhere**, so the old unconditional command died with `MODULE_NOT_FOUND` before a single spec ran.
  The source shape then needed a **second** fix that only running it could find: `pnpm --filter … run
  <script>` sets the cwd to the PACKAGE directory, so the seed's relative default for `board.data.js`
  resolved to `/repo/apps/server/reference/…` and the first live run died on `ENOENT`. Both halves live
  in `apps/web/e2e-stack/helpers/seed-shape.ts`, which is pure so the prod half — whose container is
  deliberately not started here — has a witness in `apps/web/test/seed-shape.test.ts` rather than
  shipping unmeasured. `apps/web/e2e-stack/*`, `apps/web/test/seed-shape.test.ts`,
  `apps/web/playwright.stack.config.ts`, the `e2e` job in
  `.github/workflows/ci.yml`
- ⚙️ **`WEB_ORIGIN` is a trusted auth origin — the product no longer refuses its own client** —
  better-auth's `trustedOrigins` was left at its default, the origin of `BETTER_AUTH_URL`, which is the
  SERVER's origin and not what any browser sends; a real browser at the web origin submitting the shipped
  sign-in form was answered `403 INVALID_ORIGIN` with "Invalid origin" rendered on the page. The
  configured `WEB_ORIGIN` is now appended to `trustedOrigins` — **appended, never substituted**
  (`getTrustedOrigins` pushes the `baseURL` origin first and only then appends), never a wildcard, and
  never read from the request — so a same-origin deployment is unaffected and a cross-origin one works.
  `webOrigin` is a **required** field on `AuthDeps` rather than an optional one, so the typecheck names
  every caller instead of a default hiding one. The condition was not hypothetical: `deploy/do/app.yaml`
  serves the web app and the API from different hosts, so the production deploy would have met it too.
  Trusting the origin is necessary and **not sufficient** for a genuinely cross-origin deployment — the
  CORS preflight is still unmounted on the auth catch-all, which `sign-in-flows.test.ts` pins as a known
  gap. `apps/server/src/auth/auth.ts`, `apps/server/src/index.ts`, `apps/server/src/auth/auth-dev.ts`
- ⚙️ **Operator runbook — and the one thing about prose that can be made to fail** — `docs/runbook.md`
  carries deploy (routine and first-time), the three separate settings that hold Asia/Kolkata up and why

  `scripts/check-runbook-commands.mjs`**, wired as its own CI step: each `pnpm --filter <pkg> run
  <script>`, each root `pnpm <script>` and each repo-relative script path must resolve, and the checker

- ⚙️ **`verify:projections` runs from `DATABASE_URL` alone** — it previously opened with
  `loadConfig(process.env)`, demanding `BETTER_AUTH_URL`, a 32-character `BETTER_AUTH_SECRET` and
  `WEB_ORIGIN`, none of which it reads, so the runbook's own restore drill answered `ConfigError`. A drill
  nobody can run is not a recovery procedure. It now matches the seed CLIs and `sweep-notifications`,

  only against restore scratch databases, never production, and no DDL-capable credential is added. It
  needs `CREATE SCHEMA` + `CREATE TABLE` on whatever database it verifies (it rebuilds into a
  `verify_shadow` schema), and that shadow trick is safe only while every registered projection declares
  its table — one missing from `tables` gets no shadow copy and its unqualified `TRUNCATE` resolves to
  `public`, truncating the LIVE table. It does **not** rewrite live projections; an earlier draft said it
  did, and the verifier's own docblock ("Live tables are never written") plus cases 5.55/6.117/7.63
  contradict that.
  The cost is recorded with the decision: production drift surfaces at the next monthly drill, so the
  exposure window is up to a month. `apps/server/src/tools/verify-projections.ts`, `deploy/do/README.md`
- ⚙️ **The `test-cases/` <-> test citation check (Lane F Pass 1) — the gap the coordination doc named by
  hand, made falsifiable** — a checked (`✅`) catalogue case is supposed to name the test that proves it,
  but nothing enforced the reverse: the coordination doc recorded, 2026-08-31, that no test in the repo
  cites a case id, so a `✅` could survive the deletion of its own covering test. `scripts/check-case-
  citations.mjs`, its own ungated CI job, closes the direction that was missing: every checked id in
  `test-cases/*.md` must appear as a test name or comment somewhere under a test/e2e directory. **Measured
  before choosing a mode:** 1525 checked ids, 710 already cited, 815 not — too large to backfill in this
  errand, so it runs as a RATCHET (`scripts/fixtures/check-case-citations-baseline.json`, one uncited-count
  ceiling per file) that must equal reality EXACTLY, not merely bound it: higher than reality is a
  regression (a citing test was deleted, or a new checked case shipped uncited) and fails, naming the
  offending ids; lower than reality is a STALE ceiling — also a failure, because an upper bound nobody has
  to tighten stops meaning anything and could hide a real regression on one file behind unrelated
  improvement on another if the check summed across files instead of holding each one to its own number.
  Self-tested against a planted fixture covering every id-line shape this repo's catalogues actually use
  (bare/bold/marker-first/RANGE, both before and after the id) plus a malformed-id guard (a three-part
  number like `13.2.1` is not silently truncated to a shorter real-looking id, and a cross-section range
  like `90.30–91.2` is not silently expanded or reduced to its leader), and against the ratchet's own four
  failure shapes directly. **A review round found the range shape had been silently dropping cases
  entirely**, not merely miscounting them: `- **3.89–3.94** ✅ ...` extracted only `3.89` and discarded
  the other five ids with no report at all, hiding 50 real, already-cited `✅` ids from the ratchet
  repo-wide (`03-foundation-ledger.md` alone: 10 range lines). Fixed to expand every id in a real range
  and flag a cross-section one as malformed instead. The same round found `isCited`'s boundary guard let
  a dependency version string (`"zod 3.25.76"`) satisfy a shorter id (`3.25`) with no real citation
  anywhere — fixed by also excluding a further digit after a following `.`, while still accepting an
  ordinary sentence-ending period (`case 5.18.`). The delete-a-citing-test property was also reproduced
  against REAL files, not
  only the synthetic fixture: removing both of case 11.1's citations moved `11-calendar-notifications.md`
  from 0 to 1 uncited against a committed ceiling of 0, and the check failed naming `11.1`; restored and
  re-verified clean. **Its own job carries no `needs: scope` and no docs-only `if:` gate** — the exact
  shape `check-runbook` was rewritten into after review found it silently skipped on a runbook-only diff,
  applied here before the same mistake could repeat one level up. `scripts/check-case-citations.mjs`,
  `scripts/fixtures/check-case-citations-{fixture.md,baseline.json}`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`,
  `test-cases/README.md`, cases 13.204–13.208
- ⚙️ **Responsive and contrast measured in real viewports, not read off the source** — two suites, deliberately separate because the interesting pages sit on opposite sides of a sign-in. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/` runs the five signed-out routes across three real device viewports (390 / 768 / 1512) under its own `next dev` on a dedicated port with `reuseExistingServer: false`, so it can never silently adopt whatever is listening on 3001 and report coverage of a container it did not start. `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts` covers the signed-in shell, because the defect that motivated all this — `.gn-nav` running 10px past a 390px viewport — was on the authenticated pages and on none of the signed-out ones. **Every case asserts the viewport actually took effect by reading `window.innerWidth` back inside the page**, since this repo has a recorded failure where a resize reported success while the page stayed 1512px wide. The load-bearing case is the cross-width one: a suite passing identically at 390 and 1512 has proven nothing about responsiveness. Tap targets ratchet against a committed baseline rather than asserting a floor — the count may fall, never rise — because that reading does not move with the viewport and is recorded as an owner decision in `docs/pre-launch-checklist.md`. `apps/web/playwright.viewport.config.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts`, cases 13.254–13.255

- ⚙️ **Migration-order gate — no migration may be numbered below one already on the base ref** —
  `pnpm check:migrations` compares `apps/server/migrations` against the base ref's (`origin/main`, with a
  local-`main` fallback and `--base <ref>` to override) and fails naming any migration present now and
  absent from the base whose numeric prefix is not strictly above the base's highest. **It is the one
  gate that can see this class at all**, which is why it is separate: a migration numbered below one
  already applied can never run — `node-pg-migrate`'s `checkOrder` refuses it as *"preceding already run
  migration"* — but a FRESH database applies every migration cleanly in numerical order, so the whole
  server suite is green on the broken tree by construction, and the hot stack (which migrates only at
  container start and aborts on failure) goes down instead. Measured on the real incident rather than
  reasoned about: PR #459's branch tip is **refused** against its fork point (`1789300000000` vs the
  already-present `1789400000000`) and commit `73c40ea3`, which renamed it to `1789500000000`, is
  **accepted**. The baseline is the base ref's TIP and not the merge base, deliberately: the merge base
  cannot see the case that actually broke the stack, where `main` gains a higher-numbered migration while
  a branch is open. The comparison runs over two real DIRECTORIES through one reader, so the self-test
  plants fixture directories rather than arrays — `6 planted defects caught, 5 known-good accepted`,
  including a duplicate prefix, a non-numeric prefix the runner reads as `0`, an empty baseline that must
  refuse rather than conclude, and a **17-digit `utc`-format prefix whose instant is below the highest
  present**, which a bare `Number()` would read as `2.026e16` and wrongly accept. Two honest
  non-coverage notes: it does not refuse a migration removed or renamed from the base set (a rename of a
  not-yet-applied migration is legitimate and indistinguishable from this side), and it cannot see a
  lower-numbered migration that reached the base ref before the branch existed. **The CI job passes the
  pull request's own base** (`--base origin/${{ github.base_ref }}`), not `main`: review measured that a
  branch stacked on another branch which outranks it is **accepted** against `main` and refused against
  its real base, and that shape is used here — 14 of the last 400 merges were based on a non-main
  branch. `scripts/check-migration-order.mjs`, `package.json`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, cases
  13.902–13.906

## 14. MCP server

*The assistant-facing door. An MCP server a person runs on their own machine, signed in as
themselves, whose writes are attributable in the ledger as an assistant's rather than a person's.
Mirrored by `test-cases/14-mcp-server.md`. The envelope-origin half of this feature lives in §3,
where the ledger's provenance vocabulary is defined, rather than being restated here.*

- ✅ **An assistant can operate gisik-nokma as you** — nine tools over stdio: five reads (`list_workspaces`, `list_projects`, `search_tickets`, `list_tickets`, `get_ticket`) and four writes (`create_ticket`, `transition_ticket`, `add_comment`, `assign_ticket`). It has no permissions of its own — every call is the call you would have made, judged by the same permission gate, rule engine and visibility rules as the web client, and every write is a real domain event through `POST /v1/events`. Driven end to end against the live stack: a ticket created at seq 1, a comment at seq 2, a bad transition refused as `refused by unknown-state: The workflow "Errand with waits" has no state "no-such-state".`, and the ledger reading `web 123 · server 6 · mcp 2`. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/`, cases 14.60–14.88

- ⚙️ **`@gisik-nokma/mcp-server` — the package scaffold and its configuration** (MCP plan Task 1) — a workspace package for the assistant-facing MCP door, with `loadMcpConfig(env, home)` as the one place its configuration is resolved: it REQUIRES `GISIK_SERVER_URL` and names it when absent, defaults the token cache to `~/.gisik-nokma/mcp-token`, and strips a trailing slash so later URL joins never double it. The env arrives as a PARAMETER rather than being read from `process.env`, which makes the three cases pure functions of their inputs — and, as a side effect nobody wanted, makes the file invisible to `pnpm check:env`, whose detectors all require the string `process.env` to appear (recorded in `.env.example`; owner: whoever next touches `scripts/check-env-example.mjs`). The package is registered with `check-runbook-commands.mjs`'s hand-maintained package list NOW, before Task 10's runbook entry exists, so that entry cannot land against a list that does not know the package. Cases 14.1–14.3. `packages/mcp-server/`
- ⚙️ **The MCP credential store — device-code grants and personal access tokens** (MCP plan Task 2) — two server-owned tables, `mcp_device_codes` and `mcp_tokens`, both hashed at rest (sha256 of 32 bytes from `randomBytes`; a plain hash rather than a password KDF is correct here for the same reason it is for `invite_tokens` — the input is 256 bits of uniform entropy, so there is no dictionary to stretch against). **Neither is a projection and neither is rebuildable**, deliberately: what they hold is absent from the ledger, because anyone who can read a workspace's history can read an event id and a payload. Neither name may ever appear in a projection's `tables` array — `reset()` empties what that array names, and a rebuild that emptied `mcp_tokens` would sign every MCP client out with no way to put them back. Single-use is enforced by `DELETE … RETURNING` in the statement that reads the grant, not by a read-shaped guard. A revoked token and a forged one resolve to `null` identically, because every distinction is an oracle. The user code uses a no-vowel, no-0/O/1/I/5/S alphabet so it can neither spell a word nor be transcribed into a different valid code. Cases 14.10–14.16. `apps/server/src/mcp/token-store.ts`, `apps/server/migrations/1788600000000_mcp-device-auth.sql`
- ⚙️ **The MCP device-authorization flow — four routes** (MCP plan Task 3; the DENIAL route added 2026-09-19) — `POST /v1/mcp/device/code` mints a grant, `POST /v1/mcp/device/approve` binds it to the signed-in person, `POST /v1/mcp/device/deny` **refuses** it, `POST /v1/mcp/device/token` exchanges it once. **Two of the four carry no session by design**, because the point of a device flow is that the thing asking for a credential does not have one yet; neither gives anything away, since the credential is created by the APPROVAL. **Both the approval AND the denial are cookie-authenticatable POSTs and both are inside `HTTP_ORIGIN_PROTECTED_ROUTES`** — without that, a page the victim visits could approve the ATTACKER's device code on the victim's ambient cookie, or kill a grant out from under whoever was mid-approval, and `SameSite=Lax` does not help because the product and API are same-site siblings of one registrable domain. All four are charged to a **fourth rate-limit bucket** fed by the existing `RATE_LIMIT_AUTH_MAX` (the `inviteMax` precedent, no new environment variable): before it, a route under `/v1/mcp/...` matched no bucket at all, leaving the 8-character user code an unlimited brute-force surface. That budget is also what fixes the TTL and poll interval the spec left open. **Denial is a DELETE of the grant row rather than a `denied` flag**, and that is what makes it work in both states — an unapproved code can no longer be approved, and an already-approved one can no longer be collected by the CLI. Every downstream answer is then `unknown_code`, deliberately not a distinct "denied" state that would confirm a guessed user code had been real. Cases 14.20–14.27b, 14.104–14.107b. `apps/server/src/routes/mcp-device.ts`, `apps/server/src/http/http-origin.ts`, `apps/server/src/app.ts`
- ⚙️ **A personal access token authenticates a product route — as a FALLBACK, not a branch** (MCP plan Task 4) — `requireSession` asks better-auth first, always, and consults `mcp_tokens` only when it answers null. That order is the design rather than a detail: every mobile and desktop client, and every test in this repository, authenticates with a better-auth BEARER token, so the two credential systems arrive in the same header and only the order keeps them from contending. **The order is held by a test** (14.35), not by a comment: a row keyed on the sha256 of a live session token but pointed at a different user is simultaneously a valid session for one person and a valid PAT for another, and the device-approval route makes the resolved identity observable. A forged token and a revoked one are refused identically. The cost is stated: a request that falls THROUGH to the PAT branch performs one indexed single-row write, because the lookup stamps `last_used`; a better-auth session never reaches it. Cases 14.30–14.37. `apps/server/src/auth/require-session.ts`
- ✅ **A connection can be NAMED, and the row leads with it** (door D, 2026-09-19) — `isMcpTokens`' row carries a name, set by the person who holds the token. The prototype has led each row with one all along (`Claude Desktop — Anjali`, `CI — ticket sync`, `McpTokens.dc.html:100-112`, over a second line of the token's own facts) and ours listed only the approval date, so three connections read as three dates. **The ruled-out issue-time flow is NOT required:** a name is set ABOUT a token already held, so this is a migration (`mcp_tokens.name`, `text NULL`) plus one owner-scoped write — `PUT /v1/me/mcp-tokens/:id`, the same path the DELETE owns. **`null` is NOT NAMED and it is not `''`**, the distinction `proj_projects.description` draws: `''` would be the claim "this token is called nothing" while NULL is the absence of a claim, which is what every pre-existing row has, and the row draws NOTHING rather than a blank line. The name goes AS TYPED — the route's schema refuses a blank or spaces-only name with its own sentence, and a client that trimmed would make that refusal unreachable. **⚠ The write is CSRF-protected BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by a new entry:** `HTTP_ORIGIN_PROTECTED_ROUTES` is matched by route URL with **no method in the key**, so reusing the path the DELETE already registered means there is nothing to remember and nothing to forget — asserted by a forged-`Origin` write, not by reading the set. It is a `PUT` and not a `PATCH` because `HttpClient` carries no `patch` and six files satisfy its shape; widening that interface for one route was out of the row's scope. Scoping is inside the UPDATE's own `WHERE user_id = $2`, so a stranger's row is never read on the way to refusing them, and the 404 is the same answer for "not yours" and "never existed" — the probe-resistance the DELETE argues for one function up. Cases `16.1`–`16.14`. `apps/server/migrations/1789700000000_mcp-tokens-name.sql`, `apps/server/src/mcp/token-store.ts`, `apps/server/src/routes/mcp-tokens.ts`, `apps/web/src/mcp-tokens-screen.tsx`, `apps/web/app/globals.css`
- ✅ **The MCP approval screen — the one place a person consents to an assistant acting as them** (MCP plan Task 5) — at `${WEB_ORIGIN}/mcp/authorize`, the `verificationUri` the device-code route hands out. It must live in `apps/web` and nowhere else, and that is measured rather than argued: an approval POST from a non-`WEB_ORIGIN` page answers **403 `forbidden_origin`**, while the same request from the real web origin passes the origin check. The consent is stated before the button and again in the confirmation — an assistant that acts **as this person**, with exactly their permissions, until revoked — and it is styled in `surface-sunken` rather than danger or warning, following the 404 page's recorded reasoning that an alarm colour tells the reader something has gone wrong when the product is behaving correctly. Dressing consent as an alarm trains people to click through alarms. **A `Deny` control sits beside Approve** (added 2026-09-19 with the denial route) — the counterpart a screen that explains what approving MEANS implies, since an explanation with no way to say no is not asking. The two are of **equal weight** and Deny is deliberately NOT `variant="danger"`: the product reserves that variant for the destructive actions it names, and the page's own reasoning above is that dressing a normal decision as an alarm is how people are trained to click through alarms. A denial renders its own panel and says what actually happened — the code is spent in both directions — and a 404 on the way there is presented as naming both possibilities, because "already used" and "never existed" are the same body on purpose. Case 14.100; the Deny control is cases 14.108–14.111. `apps/web/app/mcp/authorize/page.tsx`, `apps/web/test/mcp-authorize.test.tsx`
- ⚙️ **The MCP client's token cache and HTTP layer** (MCP plan Task 6) — `readCachedToken`/`writeCachedToken`/`clearCachedToken` keep the personal access token at `0600` in a `0700` directory, with the modes passed explicitly rather than left to the process umask; a credential every process on a shared machine can read is not cached, it is published. Absent, unreadable and corrupt all answer `null`, because they mean one thing to a caller and one of them is recoverable by re-running the device flow. `createGisikClient` sends a bearer credential and **never a cookie** — a bearer-only caller is outside the HTTP-Origin check by construction — carries the server's error body verbatim rather than summarising it, and **does not retry a transport failure**, since a write retried against a server that becomes reachable mid-retry double-submits. Cases 14.40–14.47. `packages/mcp-server/src/token-cache.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/http-client.ts`
- ⚙️ **The MCP device-flow login driver and its session** (MCP plan Task 7) — `runDeviceLogin` mints a code, shows it to the person BEFORE polling (they cannot approve what they have not seen), polls at **the interval the server named** rather than a constant the client carries, and gives up at a deadline instead of forever. `createSession` uses a cached token when there is one and otherwise runs the flow; a 401 re-authenticates and retries **exactly once**, because a server answering 401 to everything would otherwise spin forever and spend the device-flow rate-limit budget doing it — and the user id moves with the token, since a stale `actorId` draws an identity-spoof refusal a long way from its cause. **A 404 never re-authenticates**: the product answers it for "does not exist" and "not yours" alike, so re-auth would turn a correct visibility refusal into a login prompt. Cases 14.50–14.57. `packages/mcp-server/src/device-login.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/session.ts`
- ✅ **Five MCP read tools** (MCP plan Task 8) — `list_workspaces`, `list_projects`, `search_tickets`, `list_tickets`, `get_ticket`, each a passthrough of a route that already exists. They do not invent routes (`list_projects` reads the workspace directory and filters client-side, because the server has no projects route), do not normalise the server's shapes (`get_ticket` returns snake_case `ticket` beside camelCase `ticketState`, untouched), and do not restate the domain's `filterSpecSchema` — which is `.strict()` with SINGULAR assignee/collaborator keys, so an array is refused rather than silently matching nothing. One rule is duplicated on purpose: a filter carrying `text` is refused before the network, because the server's `invalid_filter` does not say which of the two text terms is the problem. Cases 14.60–14.66. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/read-tools.ts`
- ✅ **Four MCP write tools, and every write is attributable as an assistant's** (MCP plan Task 9) — `create_ticket`, `transition_ticket`, `add_comment`, `assign_ticket`, each building an envelope that carries `origin: 'mcp'` (owner ruling 1) and `actorId` equal to the session user, which the permission gate requires. One write path reads the result, because `POST /v1/events` answers **200 for a refusal** — refusals ride inside `results[]`, index-aligned — so a caller checking the status alone would report every refusal as a success; the rule's name and the reason's own sentence are surfaced verbatim, since a reworded refusal is a rule the product does not have. The envelope is validated in-process by the product's own `parseEvent` and registry, so a wrong payload key fails locally rather than after a round trip. **`create_ticket` takes an optional `start: 'backlog' | 'focus'`** (Ruling 51, Finding 4, final whole-branch review, 2026-09-17): the tool is a SECOND production producer of `ticket.created` beside the web dialog, and until this fix sent no `start` at all — the domain's additive-field rule then read that silence as focus, so an MCP-created ticket landed on the board rather than the backlog every other creation path defaults to (spec §1.3, amended — see Amendments, Ruling 51). An absent argument now sends an explicit `start: 'backlog'`, never an absent key; naming `'focus'` still gets exactly that. Dev seeds under `apps/server/src/seed/*` are left alone — they populate boards on purpose, and are not a production creation path. Cases 14.70–14.77, 13.607. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/write-tools.ts`
- ✅ **The MCP server runs: a `gisik-nokma-mcp` stdio entrypoint, nine registered tools, and a README that says how to install and revoke it** (MCP plan Task 10) — `register.ts` is the ONE file that knows the MCP SDK's registration API (verified against the installed `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk@1.30.0`: `inputSchema` takes a zod RAW SHAPE, which `ToolDefinition` already carries, so no `zod-to-json-schema` conversion exists anywhere); everything else speaks `ToolDefinition` and every tool test runs with no transport. A handler's throw becomes `isError` with the message unchanged, so a rule-engine refusal reaches the assistant in the product's own words. **The transport connects BEFORE any credential exists** — the device flow runs on the first tool call, because authenticating first leaves `initialize` unanswered for up to ten minutes on a fresh install and the client reports a server that failed to start (measured: 107 ms and nine tools as shipped, versus an 8-second timeout with the ordering reversed). Nothing in the package may write to stdout, which is the protocol stream; the sign-in prompt goes to stderr and a test records `process.stdout.write` to prove it. Cases 14.80–14.88. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/register.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/main.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/lazy-session.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/login-prompt.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/README.md`, the runbook's *MCP server* section


- ⚙️ **Responsive and contrast measured in real viewports, not read off the source** — two suites, deliberately separate because the interesting pages sit on opposite sides of a sign-in. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/` runs the five signed-out routes across three real device viewports (390 / 768 / 1512) under its own `next dev` on a dedicated port with `reuseExistingServer: false`, so it can never silently adopt whatever is listening on 3001 and report coverage of a container it did not start. `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts` covers the signed-in shell, because the defect that motivated all this — `.gn-nav` running 10px past a 390px viewport — was on the authenticated pages and on none of the signed-out ones. **Every case asserts the viewport actually took effect by reading `window.innerWidth` back inside the page**, since this repo has a recorded failure where a resize reported success while the page stayed 1512px wide. The load-bearing case is the cross-width one: a suite passing identically at 390 and 1512 has proven nothing about responsiveness. Tap targets ratchet against a committed baseline rather than asserting a floor — the count may fall, never rise — because that reading does not move with the viewport and is recorded as an owner decision in `docs/pre-launch-checklist.md`. `apps/web/playwright.viewport.config.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts`, cases 13.254–13.255
- ⚙️ **Security headers on both apps, and the two that are deliberately absent** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — until now the product sent exactly one, `Strict-Transport-Security`. Both apps now send `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and `X-Frame-Options: DENY` unconditionally; the API sends `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and the web app `strict-origin-when-cross-origin`, because a browser never navigates FROM an API response while a page whose links stop carrying their origin breaks flows that read it. The server's hook sits outside the `options.security` block so the ~60 test call sites that omit it can still witness the headers. **`Content-Security-Policy` and `Permissions-Policy` are deferred, and their absence is asserted** — both are statements about what a document may do, and for the web app a policy is a real design problem whose failure mode is breaking in production only. The web app also stops advertising `X-Powered-By: Next.js` — found by curling the running container, since a sweep for MISSING headers cannot notice one that is present and unwanted. Cases 13.256–13.261, 13.263. `apps/server/src/http/security-headers.ts`, `apps/web/next.config.ts`
- ⚙️ **A mobile build that would inline a working credential refuses to run** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — `EXPO_PUBLIC_DEV_TOKEN` is substituted into the bundle at build time, so a build carrying one publishes a bearer credential in plain text to whoever holds the binary. `apps/mobile/scripts/check-dev-credentials.mjs` refuses BY DEFAULT and admits it only with an explicit `GISIK_ALLOW_DEV_TOKEN=1`, because a control that needs a "this is a release" signal to be present fails open the moment that signal is absent. It scans `.env*` files as well as the environment — the way `EXPO_PUBLIC_*` is normally set, and the blind spot of an env-only guard. Wired into the `build` script with `&&`, self-testing before it certifies anything. Cases 12.390–12.393. `apps/mobile/scripts/check-dev-credentials.mjs`, `apps/mobile/package.json`
- ✅ **The phone's help screen follows the device's light/dark setting** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — it resolved every colour through `colorOf('light', role)` until now, so a dark-mode phone got light colours. `useColorScheme()` selects the palette and `app.json` declares `userInterfaceStyle: automatic`, without which the OS never reports dark and the hook answers light forever. The palette lives in a sibling `.ts` with no react-native import, which is what makes both branches testable in a node runner without a simulator. **That sibling now carries the screen's TYPEFACE decision too (2026-09-15), from the port of the desktop prototype's fonts across all three clients:** `monoFamilyFor(os)` returns `Menlo` on iOS and the generic `monospace` elsewhere, replacing the screen's hardcoded `'Courier'` — a narrow typewriter face iOS ships for compatibility that no part of this design asked for. React Native takes ONE family name and not a CSS stack, so the prototype's `ui-monospace, Menlo, monospace` has to become a branch, and it is taken here rather than with `Platform.select` for the same reason the palette is: that call would drag `react-native` into the node runner this module exists to stay out of, so the screen passes `Platform.OS` in. **Body text deliberately gets NO `fontFamily` and that absence IS the port:** the prototype's stack begins `-apple-system`, a CSS keyword meaningless inside a `StyleSheet`, and React Native already paints unstyled text in the platform's system font — San Francisco on iOS, precisely what that keyword resolves to. Declaring the stack would name six families the platform cannot find and fall back silently. Cases 12.394–12.397. `apps/mobile/src/screens/help-theme.ts`, `apps/mobile/src/screens/help-screen.tsx`, `apps/mobile/app.json`
- ✅ **Every standalone control meets the 44px touch floor** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — the shared `.gn-button` was `340x38`, six pixels short *everywhere it is used*; the view tabs `55x21`; the sign-in inputs `340x21`. All now `min-height: 2.75rem`, which took the signed-out viewport ratchet from 60 offenders to 42 and `/sign-in` to 0 at 390, 768 and 1512. `/help`'s table-of-contents links went to 24px — WCAG 2.5.8's floor — and deliberately not 44px, because nineteen entries at 44px is 836px tall on a phone: dense in-list links use 24, standalone controls 44. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/tap-target-baseline.json`, `packages/ui/src/ui.css`, `apps/web/app/globals.css`
- ⚙️ **The signed-in shell's tap targets have a regression guard** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — `e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts` measured overflow only, so the pre-launch review's counts for the board lived in a prose note no run could contradict. It now carries the same sweep and ratchet as the unauthed suite, importing them from `apps/web/e2e-viewport/tap-targets.ts` — a plain module, because importing them from the spec made Playwright load that spec's tests into the other config. `/my-work` at 390px went 9 offenders → 7 when the nav's links reached 44px, then → 6 once `a.gn-nav__inbox` got a MIN-WIDTH as well (it was `39x44`: tall enough and six pixels too narrow, which re-reading the count could never have shown) and the six in-list title buttons reached 24px. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/tap-targets.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/tap-target-baseline.json`
- ⚙️ **The tap-target checker now AGREES with the product's own 24/44 rule — two floors, and the 44px one still bites** (owner ruling, 2026-09-20) — the sweep enforced a FLAT 44 on every control while `packages/ui/src/ui.css` had been arguing the 24px WCAG 2.5.8 floor in its own words at four separate sites (`.gn-attention__title`, `.gn-help__nav-link`, `.gn-help__group-toggle`, `.gn-help__pager-link`), so the sweep was counting all four as violations of a rule the product had deliberately declined. The owner, verbatim: *"let the checker accept 24 for in-list controls, keep 44 for standalone buttons."* `DENSE_IN_LIST` in `apps/web/e2e-viewport/tap-targets.ts` is a DECLARED table — control, container, the `ui.css` rule that makes the call, and why — every offender now carries the floor it missed, and a stale entry (a container that renders while its control selector matches nothing) is reported rather than dying silently. `apps/web/test/tap-target-density.test.ts` pins the two constants **on the gate**, and it reddens if `STANDALONE_FLOOR_PX` is ever changed to 24 — the blanket-24 this change exists to prevent. Membership is declared rather than derived because three structural predicates were tried against the real markup and each is wrong in the dangerous direction: "inside a `<ul>`" would have re-judged `.gn-board-card__move` (44px, inside `ul.gn-board__cards`) at 24; "a direct child of an `<li>`" misses `.gn-help__group-toggle` (in a `<div>`) and `.gn-help__pager-link` (an inline link in a `<p>`); "≥2 sibling controls of the same class" would have caught a board card's own arrow pair. Measured at `99f2a780` before the split: `/help` read **25 / 23 / 25** offenders at 390 / 768 / 1512 against recorded baselines of **10 / 16 / 16**, every one of them an in-list control the CSS had already given the 24px floor; after the split those three routes read **0** and the baselines were re-recorded DOWN to it — the falling direction only, which is the one `recordTapBaseline` blesses. **⚠ And the reason nobody had seen it: NEITHER viewport suite was on any gate.** `pnpm turbo run build test typecheck` defined no task that reaches them and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` ran `test:e2e:stack` only — the string `viewport` did not occur in that file, measured with a positive control in the same invocation — so the ratchet rose 10 → 25 and sat red on `main`, seen by nobody, for as long as the topic sidebar has existed. **✅ TAKEN 2026-09-20 — see the `viewport` job bullet below (case 16.59).** The signed-in baseline (`/my-work`, 6) was NOT re-measured: `e2e-stack` runs against the live stack on `:3001`, which is executor 1's worktree, so a count taken from here would not be a measurement of this tree. It is left at 6 — untouched is not loosened — and the attention strip's titles are exactly what the split now classifies in-list. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/tap-targets.ts`, `apps/web/test/tap-target-density.test.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/tap-target-baseline.json`
- ⚙️ **The viewport suite RUNS on CI's gate, and the check is on what the job does rather than what it is called** (owner ruling *"wire it into the gate"*, 2026-09-20 — case 16.59) — `apps/web/playwright.viewport.config.ts` drives a real Chromium at 390 / 768 / 1512 against a `next dev` built from the working tree, and until now it was reachable only by an explicit `-c` spelling that no turbo task defined and that `ci.yml` never named: **`viewport` occurred 0 times in that file**, with a positive control in the same invocation. Every red the ratchet ever recorded was invisible for exactly that long. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` now carries a `viewport` job whose steps invoke `pnpm run test:e2e:viewport`, and `gate` waits on it, so a green merge decision requires a real browser. **The job-graph test asserts the COMMAND, not the key**: a `viewport:` job whose steps run nothing satisfies every name-shaped check while covering nothing, and then reads as coverage — strictly worse than no job at all, which is the reasoning the `e2e` job's own comment already gives for refusing a file-presence gate. Mutation-witnessed both ways (delete it from `gate`'s `needs` → red naming that list; change the step to `echo` → red on the command). **Deliberately NOT a turbo task**: a turbo verdict can be replayed from cache, and `turbo.json` already records `#270` reaching `main` behind a replayed green log. The suite is self-contained — its own `next dev` on 3021 with `reuseExistingServer: false`, and 16 `page.route` stubs — so the job needs no Postgres, no compose stack and no secrets. Measured: **67 s wall** on a warm `.next` (31 passed / 0 failed / 2 skipped); the cold figure was **not** measured. ⚠ It has never executed in CI — Actions on this account fails every job before its first step, the owner-waived billing block, **831 runs since 2026-09-07 with zero successes (829 failure, 2 cancelled)** — so the command was run locally at this head. `apps/web/playwright.viewport.config.ts`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `apps/server/test/ci/ci-workflow-shape.test.ts`
- ⚙️ **The body-text floor SPLITS by a declared membership rule instead of dropping** (owner ruling, 2026-09-20 — case 16.60) — `/help` at 390px was the one red the wiring above would have imported: 17 offenders, every one an uppercase eyebrow LABEL (`p.gn-help__eyebrow` 12.48px, `p.gn-help__section-eyebrow` 13px), both owner-directed guide values (`c21246b0`, *"lets move the styling towards the guide"*), both inside the `0.78rem-1.3rem` band the type-scale file itself calls the legitimate scale, both above the 12px label floor — red only for being `<p>`. **A `<p>` is in the body sweep's selector and NOT in the label sweep's**, so deleting it from one without adding it to the other would have left it with **no floor at all, silently** — the ruling's own question, checked rather than assumed, and the reason the fix is a **SECOND SELECTOR** rather than an exemption. `type-scale.ts`'s `LABEL_IN_BODY` declares which classes are labels and why, in `DENSE_IN_LIST`'s shape (label, container, witness, the rule that sets the size). **Neither the floor nor the products changed:** section 4's 16px stays hard for anything undeclared, `type-scale-baseline.json` is untouched and every key in it is still 0. Two assertions exist because a declaration table creates two ways to be green while covering nothing — the hand-off is measured from BOTH ends (what section 4 handed over must equal what section 4b received by declaration; an exemption leaves the element in NEITHER population, green), and a renamed entry reddens rather than dying silently. All three mutations witnessed, each isolated: undeclare a label → section 4 names it at 16px; rename one → the stale guard fires at a width where section 4 is silent; collapse the selector to its base → *"section 4 handed 17 … but the label sweep received 0"*. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/type-scale.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/responsive.spec.ts`
- ⚙️ **A type-scale floor, and the sweep that could not see the text it was about** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — the other half of the ruling whose tap-target half shipped in #328. `responsive.spec.ts` had swept body copy against 16px since item 7, but its population is `p, li, dd, dt, td, figcaption, label`, and every element the pre-launch finding named — `gn-board-card__provenance`, the chips — is a `span` or a `div`. The reading came from one manual measurement and had no guard. `e2e-viewport/type-scale.ts` sweeps that population as a plain module (not a spec: importing a sweep from a spec loads its `test()` registrations into the other config), ratcheted per route in both suites. **The floor is 12px and it is NOT a WCAG number** — no success criterion sets a minimum font size; 1.4.4 is resize-to-200%, 1.4.12 is text spacing. It is the bottom rung of this product's own scale, seventeen rules at `0.75rem`, and the checklist itself judged the 12.5px and 12.8px labels legitimate. Five declarations sat under it across the three shipped stylesheets and are now at `0.75rem`. **One of the five is witnessed by a ratchet, and the other four were chased until unwitnessable was a MEASUREMENT** — `.ticket-type-list__badge`, 4 → 0 on `/settings/ticket-types`; the other four render only on the workflow editor canvas, inside a description editor, or on a card with an assignee (no seeded card has one), and are fixed but unproven: the workflow editor renders no canvas at all at 390px (`flowNodes: 0`), and the seed assigns nothing. The witnessed rule also carries a `MUST_EXAMINE` coverage assertion, because a ratchet at 0 for a rule that stopped rendering reads exactly like a ratchet at 0 for a healthy one. Widening the population to `a`/`button` put the checklist's own named element under a guard for the first time: `button.gn-board-card__provenance`, 12.48px x26, above the floor and previously invisible to every sweep because it is a `<button>`. The claim that justifies two sweeps rather than one — disjoint populations — is measured, not asserted: an element matching both selectors lands in `bodyOwned` and reddens by name. Cases 13.264–13.265. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/type-scale.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts`, `packages/ui/src/ui.css`, `packages/editor/src/styles/editor.css`
- ✅ **Every control renders in the product's font, not the browser's** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — `<button>` with no `font` rule inherited Chrome's UA default (`13.3333px Arial`), so thirty controls were painted in a typeface the product never chose. `button { font: inherit; }` in `apps/web/app/globals.css`, an ELEMENT selector at specificity 0,0,1 so every class rule still wins — which is what keeps the owner's "and no further" true rather than promised: `.gn-board-card__provenance` stays at 12.48px x26 and every non-13.3333px bucket in the sweep is unmoved. The count grew 26 → 30 by measuring before writing the rule: `/sign-in`'s two `.gn-view-tabs` tabs were never in the signed-in enumeration, and **`/help`'s two `.gn-help__tab` were at 16px in Arial** — `ui.css:1321` sets `font-size: 1rem` and no `font-family`, so their SIZE was right and no font-size instrument could ever have seen them. *(`.gn-help__tab` no longer exists as of 2026-09-15: `/help`'s document switch is the shared `SegmentedControl`, and the one remaining user of the class — `apps/desktop`'s help toggle — carries `.gn-help__toggle`. The finding and its argument stand; only the selector it was found on has been renamed out from under this sentence.)* **30 is a floor**: ten of nineteen routes are swept by neither suite. Guarded by a font-FAMILY reading rather than an assertion about the `13.3333px` bucket, because the bucket form passes over the `/help` pair and would pass again the day a UA constant changes. Cases 13.266–13.267. `apps/web/app/globals.css`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/type-scale.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/responsive.spec.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts`
- ✅ **The magic link lands you in the app, not on the API's 404** (owner-dispatched P1, 2026-09-08) — `sign-in/page.tsx` handed better-auth a RELATIVE `callbackURL`, which the browser resolves at token-verification time while sitting on the API origin (that is where the emailed link points), so every magic-link sign-in ended on raw JSON. Measured: `:3001/workspaces` 200, `:3000/workspaces` 404. **Not dev-only** — `deploy/do/app.yaml` ships production as two sibling subdomains, the same split, so this was every new user's first screen. The callback is now absolute on `window.location.origin` (the web origin by definition, with no env var to drift), for the magic link **and for Google, which had the identical shape and was not in the report**. `?next=` is constrained to a same-site path in the same change, because making the callback absolute would otherwise have turned an existing client-side open-redirect into an authenticated one. The e2e guard is the other half: its only assertion was that the path no longer contained `/sign-in`, **which a JSON 404 satisfies** — it asserted the user left, never that they arrived. It now asserts the landing ORIGIN. Cases 13.268–13.272. `apps/web/app/sign-in/page.tsx`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/helpers/sign-in.ts`, `apps/server/test/auth/trusted-origins.test.ts`, `apps/web/test/sign-in.test.tsx`
- ⚙️ **One rate limiter on `/api/auth/*`, and it is ours** (controller ruling, 2026-09-08) — better-auth ships its own limiter and it could not tell clients apart here: `createRateLimitKey(ip ?? NO_TRUSTED_IP_KEY, path)` with `getIP()` falsy is **one shared bucket per path for everybody**, so one person retrying sign-in could refuse every other user. Measured on the dev stack with two clients on different addresses and no proxy in the path: the one that had spent nothing was refused on its first request. `auth.ts` now sets `rateLimit: { enabled: false }` and our limiter — which keys per client address under `TRUST_PROXY`, taking the RIGHTMOST `X-Forwarded-For` entry — is the single authority. Chosen over fixing better-auth's IP resolution because that means reading a caller-written header, putting a spoofable bucket beside a hardened one on the same path; our coverage is already a strict superset. **`RATE_LIMIT_AUTH_MAX` is untouched and finally means what everyone assumed it meant.** The named loss: better-auth's per-path tightening on the magic-link route (`window || 60`, `max || 5`, which overrides the generic `/sign-in*` 10s/3) goes away; a sign-in-specific bucket in our limiter is a recorded follow-up, **now built — see the entry below**. Cases 13.273–13.274. `apps/server/src/auth/auth.ts`, `apps/server/test/http/security-middleware.test.ts`

- ⚙️ **A tighter bucket for the sign-in family, charged on top of the general one** (2026-09-08, the follow-up the ruling above recorded) — disabling better-auth's limiter took a named loss: it gave `/sign-in*` and `/magic-link*` tighter rules than its global default, so afterwards the whole mount sat behind one flat `RATE_LIMIT_AUTH_MAX` and a client could spend its entire auth allowance on sign-in attempts. A fifth bucket now repays it, and it is the first that classifies by PATH rather than by Fastify route pattern — every auth path arrives under the wildcard mount as `/api/auth/*`, so telling a sign-in from a session read means reading the caller-written `request.url`. **Additive, never replacing:** charging a matching path to the new bucket *instead of* the general one would hand a client `authMax + signInMax` where it gets `authMax` today, a loosening under a parity headline; charging both means the smaller budget binds, and a path the matcher misses degrades to exactly today's behaviour. The budget is `signInMaxFor(authMax)` — half, so at the shipped `RATE_LIMIT_AUTH_MAX=10` it is **5**, while the dev stack's 60 leaves the e2e suite the headroom its single proxied client key needs. **That 5 is NOT parity, and an earlier version of this entry said it was.** better-auth keyed per (ip, *path*), so `/sign-in/magic-link` and `/magic-link/verify` each had their own 5-per-60s budget — five complete sign-in cycles — while this is one bucket across the whole family and a cycle costs two of it: **two complete cycles per window, measured, so between 2.5x and 5x tighter than what it replaces.** The direction is the one the ruling wanted and it is kept, but it is named as a tightening beyond the authorised parity rather than sold as parity, and the cost is named with it: where one client key covers many people — a NAT'd office egress, or the single proxied key the dev stack itself has — the whole key gets two sign-in cycles a minute. Under `TRUST_PROXY` in production the key is the real client address, so there it is per-person. No new environment variable (the `inviteMax`/`mcpMax` precedent) and `RATE_LIMIT_AUTH_MAX` still untouched. The matcher deliberately **over-matches** — dot segments, doubled slashes, trailing slashes, mixed case and percent-encoding all resolve toward matching — because a false positive costs the caller one unit of its own budget on a path that then 404s, while a false negative hands it a budget twice the size; only one of those is a bypass. `/phone-number/*` is deliberately outside it: better-auth bounded it at 10/60s, exactly the shipped default, so nothing was lost there and including it would exceed parity rather than restore it. `retry-after` on this path is a LOWER bound, deliberately and now pinned by a case: the two buckets' windows can open at different instants, so a caller told 1 second was measured being refused for 59 more — a hint that is too short, never a bound that admits too much. Cases 13.275–13.280. `apps/server/src/http/rate-limit.ts`, `apps/server/src/app.ts`, `apps/server/src/index.ts`

- ✅ **You can see and revoke the assistants connected to your account** (MCP plan Task 12, owner ruling 5) — `/mcp/tokens` lists them and revokes one in a click, replacing the plan's default of a hand-written `DELETE`. It is a PER-USER screen, not a workspace setting: an MCP token carries your permissions in every workspace you belong to, so a path with a workspace in it would say something false about what revoking does. The row shows when the connection was approved and when the assistant last used it — "Never used" when it has not, rather than a date invented to fill the column — and the page says why there is nothing else: only a one-way hash of the connection is stored. Showing last-used was an owner ruling (2026-09-08); the column was already being written on every resolution, so only the display was open. Revoking ends access at the assistant's NEXT request rather than interrupting it mid-task. `DELETE /v1/me/mcp-tokens/:id` is scoped to the caller inside the statement and answers the same 404 for "not yours" and "does not exist", so it is not an oracle for which tokens exist. Cases 14.90–14.103. **Round 21 (2026-09-16), owner: "lets move MCP tokens to the account page. as this should not be scoped to a workspace." The screen now lives on `/account`** as its "Connected assistants" section, and left BOTH workspace-scoped surfaces it had drifted into: the workspace home's section cards (five doors down to four) and `/settings`' sidebar tab (three tabs down to two, `SettingsTab` down to `'ticket-types' | 'workflows'`). This does not reverse Round 19d's ruling that it "should live inside settings" — it moves it to the settings shell it belongs to, since `/account` IS a person's settings and `/settings` is a workspace's. The same round made the nav's Settings link workspace-conditional, which is what makes leaving that shell safe: `/settings` with no `?workspaceId=` is no longer a destination the bar offers, so the tab that used to be the only thing working there is not stranding anyone. `/mcp/tokens` still resolves and still mounts the same shared component — nobody asked for a URL to die. `McpTokensScreen` gained one `variant` prop (`'page' | 'account'`) deriving three things from a single decision so they cannot disagree: the heading class, the card's max-width, and — the one that is not cosmetic — the sign-in `next` path, since the signed-out branch promises "you land back here" and a hard-coded `/mcp/tokens` would have made that sentence false on `/account`. Case 13.415. `apps/server/src/routes/mcp-tokens.ts`, `apps/web/app/mcp/tokens/page.tsx`, `apps/web/src/mcp-tokens-screen.tsx`, `apps/web/app/account/page.tsx`, `apps/web/app/settings/page.tsx`
- ⚙️ **The HTTP-Origin CSRF control covers all four mutating methods, not just POST** (found by MCP plan Task 12) — `httpOriginRefused` returned early on `method !== 'POST'` under a comment reading "mutations only". Every protected route happened to be a POST, so the control looked complete while being unable to protect a PUT, PATCH or DELETE added to the set; the forged-`Origin` DELETE reached `requireSession`, which answered 401 for the wrong reason. `security-middleware.test.ts`'s route table now carries a method column, because a table that cannot express a non-POST route cannot catch it. `apps/server/src/http/http-origin.ts`, case 14.95
- ⚙️ **The task ledger refuses a row that cites a PR which never merged** (2026-09-08) — `done` rows are validated by sha reachability, but a non-`done` row requires no evidence by design, so a wrong status carrying a stale PR number was invisible to every other check in the gate. It went wrong for real: `mcp-server/12` read `in-flight 324` while the work had landed through #329, because merging #323 deleted #324's base branch and GitHub auto-closed it. Case 13.262. `scripts/check-task-ledger.mjs`
